- [![Relief from Trowulan: Countryside, walled kampung, ramparts, and temple.](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/Plate_V_No_22_Relief_of_Majapahit_in_Trowulan.jpg/960px-Plate_V_No_22_Relief_of_Majapahit_in_Trowulan.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Plate_V_No_22_Relief_of_Majapahit_in_Trowulan.jpg "Relief from Trowulan: Countryside, walled kampung, ramparts, and temple.")

Relief from Trowulan: Countryside, walled _[kampung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kampong "Kampong")_, [ramparts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rampart_(fortification) "Rampart (fortification)"), and temple.

- [![A relief from Trowulan, showing several walled compounds and a larger wall to their north.](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a1/Reli%C3%ABf_uit_Trowoelan_bij_Modjokerto%2C_KITLV_183371.tiff/lossy-page1-250px-Reli%C3%ABf_uit_Trowoelan_bij_Modjokerto%2C_KITLV_183371.tiff.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Reli%C3%ABf_uit_Trowoelan_bij_Modjokerto,_KITLV_183371.tiff "A relief from Trowulan, showing several walled compounds and a larger wall to their north.")

A relief from Trowulan, showing several walled compounds and a larger wall to their north.

The first European record about Majapahit came from the travel log of the [Italian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italy "Italy") [Mattiussi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Odoric_of_Pordenone "Odoric of Pordenone"), a Franciscan friar. In his book _Travels of Friar [Odoric of Pordenone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Odoric_of_Pordenone "Odoric of Pordenone")_, he visited several places in today's Indonesia: [Sumatra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sumatra "Sumatra"), [Java](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Java "Java"), and [Banjarmasin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banjarmasin "Banjarmasin") in Borneo, between 1318 and 1330. He was sent by the Pope to launch a mission into the Asian interiors. In 1318 he departed from [Padua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Padua "Padua"), crossed the [Black Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Sea "Black Sea") into [Persia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persia "Persia"), all the way across [Calcutta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calcutta "Calcutta"), [Madras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madras "Madras"), and [Sri Lanka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sri_Lanka "Sri Lanka"). He then headed to [Nicobar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicobar_Islands "Nicobar Islands") island to Sumatra, before visiting Java and Banjarmasin. He returned to Italy by land through Vietnam, China, all the way through the [silkroad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silkroad "Silkroad") to Europe in 1330.

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fb/Close-up_of_warriors_from_KITLV_87835_-_Isidore_van_Kinsbergen_-_Relief_on_Tjandi_Panataran_near_Blitar_-_Before_1900.jpg/250px-Close-up_of_warriors_from_KITLV_87835_-_Isidore_van_Kinsbergen_-_Relief_on_Tjandi_Panataran_near_Blitar_-_Before_1900.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Close-up_of_warriors_from_KITLV_87835_-_Isidore_van_Kinsbergen_-_Relief_on_Tjandi_Panataran_near_Blitar_-_Before_1900.jpg) An armored horse rider, two armored warriors, and a noble figure. From Penataran temple, ca. 1347.

In his book, he mentioned that he visited Java without explaining the exact place he had visited. He said that king of Java ruled over seven other kings (vassals).[&#8202;](#ref-back-94-0)[\[note 12\]](#refnote-94) He described the richness of this island, and recorded that the [Mongol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_Empire "Mongol Empire") emperors had repeatedly tried to attack Java, but always ended up in failure and managed to be sent back to the mainland.[&#8202;](#ref-back-95-0)[\[note 13\]](#refnote-95) His full account is as follows:[&#8202;](#ref-back-:1-0)[\[83\]](#refnote-:1):87–89

> In the neighbourhood of that realm (Sumatra) is a great island, Java by name, which hath a compass of a good three thousand miles. And the king of it hath subject to himself seven crowned kings. Now this island is populous exceedingly, and is the second best of all islands that exist. For in it grow camphor, [cubebs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cubeb "Cubeb"), cardamoms, [nutmegs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nutmeg "Nutmeg"), and many other precious spices. It hath also very great store of all victuals save wine.
>
> The king of this island hath a palace which is truly marvellous. For it is very great, and hath very great staircases, broad and lofty, and the steps thereof are of gold and silver alternately. Likewise the pavement of the palace hath one tile of gold and the other of silver, and the wall of the same is on the inside plated all over with plates of gold, on which are sculptured knights all of gold, which have great golden circles round their heads, such as we give in these parts to the figures of saints. And these circles are all beset with precious stones. Moreover, the ceiling is all of pure gold, and to speak briefly, this palace is richer and finer than any existing at this day in the world.
>
> Now the Great Khan of Cathay many a time engaged in war with this king; but this king always vanquished and got the better of him. And many other things there be which I write not.

The Javanese kingdom mentioned in this record is Majapahit, and the time of his visit was in 1321 during the reign of [Jayanegara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jayanegara "Jayanegara") (1309–1328).[&#8202;](#ref-back-:1-1)[\[83\]](#refnote-:1):87[&#8202;](#ref-back-:18-1)[\[81\]](#refnote-:18):459[&#8202;](#ref-back-:19-0)[\[84\]](#refnote-:19):322

In _[Yingya Shenglan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yingya_Shenglan "Yingya Shenglan")_–a record about [Zheng He](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zheng_He "Zheng He")'s expedition (1405–1433)– [Ma Huan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ma_Huan "Ma Huan") describes the culture, customs, various social and economic aspects of _Chao-Wa_ (Java) during Majapahit period.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Chao-Wa-2)[\[27\]](#refnote-Chao-Wa) Ma Huan visited Java during Zheng He's 4th expedition in 1413, during the reign of Majapahit king [Wikramawardhana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikramawardhana "Wikramawardhana"). He describes his travel to Majapahit capital, first, he arrived at the port of _Tu-pan_ ( [Tuban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuban "Tuban")) where he saw large numbers of Chinese settlers migrated from [Guangdong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guangdong "Guangdong") and Chou Chang. Then he sailed east to thriving new trading town of _Ko-erh-hsi_ ( [Gresik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gresik "Gresik")), _Su-pa-erh-ya_ ( [Surabaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surabaya "Surabaya")), and then sailing inland into the river by smaller boat to the southwest until reached the river port of _Chang-ku_ (Changgu).[&#8202;](#ref-back-Chao-Wa-3)[\[27\]](#refnote-Chao-Wa) Continued travel by land to the southwest he arrived in _Man-che-po-I_ (Majapahit), where the king stay. There are about 200 or 300 foreign families resides in this place, with seven or eight leaders to serve the king. The climate is constantly hot, like summer.[&#8202;](#ref-back-yingyai-2)[\[26\]](#refnote-yingyai)
He describes the king's costumes; wearing a crown of gold leaves and flowers or sometimes without any headgear; bare-chested without wearing a robe, the bottom parts wears two sashes of embroidered silk. Additional silk rope is looped around the waist as a belt, and the belt is inserted with one or two short blades, called _pu-la-t'ou_ ( _belati_ or more precisely [kris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kris "Kris") dagger), walking barefoot. When travelling outside, the king rides an [elephant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elephant "Elephant") or an ox-drawn carriage.[&#8202;](#ref-back-yingyai-3)[\[26\]](#refnote-yingyai)

- [![A statue of bronze elephant with riders, made in the 13th–14th century East Java (Singhasari and Majapahit era).](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4c/Bronze_elephant_with_riders%2C_East_Java.jpg/250px-Bronze_elephant_with_riders%2C_East_Java.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Bronze_elephant_with_riders,_East_Java.jpg "A statue of bronze elephant with riders, made in the 13th–14th century East Java (Singhasari and Majapahit era).")

A statue of bronze elephant with riders, made in the 13th–14th century East Java (Singhasari and Majapahit era).

- [![Gold Kāla rattle, 11th–14th century, from Malang, East Java.](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/Kala_Demon_Rattle_made_in_Malang%2C_Java%2C_Indonesia%2C_Accession_No._2008.21.1.jpg/500px-Kala_Demon_Rattle_made_in_Malang%2C_Java%2C_Indonesia%2C_Accession_No._2008.21.1.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Kala_Demon_Rattle_made_in_Malang,_Java,_Indonesia,_Accession_No._2008.21.1.jpg "Gold Kāla rattle, 11th–14th century, from Malang, East Java.")

Gold [Kāla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C4%81la "Kāla") rattle, 11th–14th century, from Malang, East Java.

Commoners' clothing for men is without headgear and women arrange their hair like a bun secured with a hairpin. They wore clothing on the upper body and wrapped unstitched fabrics around the bottom part,[&#8202;](#ref-back-98-0)[\[note 14\]](#refnote-98) Men, from a boy aged three to elders, slipped _pu-la-t'ou_ (dagger) in their belt. The dagger are made entirely of steel, featuring intricate motifs. The handles are made of materials such as gold, [rhino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhino "Rhino") horn, or [ivory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivory "Ivory"), carved with a depiction of a human or a demon.[&#8202;](#ref-back-yingyai-4)[\[26\]](#refnote-yingyai)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Chao-Wa-4)[\[27\]](#refnote-Chao-Wa)

Majapahit people, men and women, favoured their head.[&#8202;](#ref-back-99-0)[\[note 15\]](#refnote-99) If someone was touched on his head, or if there is a misunderstanding or argument when drunk, they will instantly draw their knives and stab each other.[&#8202;](#ref-back-yingyai-5)[\[26\]](#refnote-yingyai)

The population of the country did not have a bed or chair to sit and to eat they do not use a spoon or chopsticks. Men and women enjoy chewing [betel nut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Betel_nut "Betel nut") mixed with, [betel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Betel "Betel") leaves, and white chalk made from ground mussels shells.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Chao-Wa-5)[\[27\]](#refnote-Chao-Wa) They eat [rice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rice "Rice") for the meal, first, they took a scoop of water and soak betel in their mouth, then wash their hands and sit down to make a circle; getting a plate of rice soaked in butter (probably [coconut milk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coconut_milk "Coconut milk")) and gravy, and eat using hands to lift the rice and put it in their mouth. When receiving guests, they will offer the guests, not the [tea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tea "Tea"), but with betel nut.[&#8202;](#ref-back-yingyai-6)[\[26\]](#refnote-yingyai)

The population consisted of Muslim merchants from the west (Arab and Muslim Indians, but mostly those from Muslim states in Sumatra), Chinese (claimed to be descendants of [Tang dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tang_dynasty "Tang dynasty")), and unrefined locals. The king held annual jousting tournaments.[&#8202;](#ref-back-yingyai-7)[\[26\]](#refnote-yingyai):45 About the marriage rituals; the groom pays a visit to the house of the bride's family, the marriage union is consummated. Three days later, the groom escorts his bride back to his home, where the man's family beat drums and brass gongs, blowing pipes made from coconut shells ( _senterewe_), beating a drum made from [bamboo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bamboo "Bamboo") tubes (probably a kind of bamboo [gamelan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamelan "Gamelan") or [kolintang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kolintang "Kolintang")), and light fireworks.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Chao-Wa-6)[\[27\]](#refnote-Chao-Wa) Escorted in front, behind, and around by men holding short blades and shields. While the bride is a matted-hair woman, with an uncovered body and barefooted. She wraps herself in embroidered silk, wears a necklace around her neck adorned with gold beads, and [bracelets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bracelet "Bracelet") on her wrist with ornaments of gold, silver and other precious ornaments. Family, friends and neighbours decorate a decorative boat with betel leaf, [areca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Areca "Areca") nut, reeds and flowers are sewn, and arrange a party to welcome the couple on such a festive occasion. When the groom arrives home, the gong and drum are sounded, they will drink wine (possibly [arrack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arrack "Arrack") or [tuak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuak "Tuak")) and play music. After a few days, the festivities end.[&#8202;](#ref-back-yingyai-8)[\[26\]](#refnote-yingyai)

About the burial rituals, the dead body was left on the beach or empty land to be devoured by dogs (for lower-class), cremated, or committed into the waters (Javanese: _Larung_). The upper-class performed [sati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sati_(practise) "Sati (practise)"), a suicide ritual by widowed wives, concubines or female servants, through [self immolation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self_immolation "Self immolation") by throwing themselves into flaming cremation fire.[&#8202;](#ref-back-yingyai-9)[\[26\]](#refnote-yingyai)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Chao-Wa-7)[\[27\]](#refnote-Chao-Wa)

In this record, Ma Huan also describes a musical troupe travelling during full moon nights. Numbers of people holding shoulders creating an unbroken line while singing and chanting in unison, while the families whose houses being visited would give them copper coins or gifts. He also describes a class of artisans that draws various images on paper and give a theatrical performance. The narrator tells the story of legends, tales and romance drawn upon a screen of rolled paper.[&#8202;](#ref-back-yingyai-10)[\[26\]](#refnote-yingyai) This kind of performance is identified as _[wayang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayang "Wayang") bébér_, an art of story-telling that has survived for many centuries in Java.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Chao-Wa-8)[\[27\]](#refnote-Chao-Wa)

Portuguese diplomat [Tomé Pires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom%C3%A9_Pires "Tomé Pires"), who visited the archipelago in 1512, recorded the culture of Java in the late Majapahit era, after his visit to the island between March and June 1513.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:3-1)[\[77\]](#refnote-:3):xxv Pires' account tells about the lords and nobles in Java. They are described as:

> ...tall and handsome, lavishly adorned, and they have richly caparisoned horses. They use krises, swords, and lances of many kinds, all inlaid with gold. They are great hunters and horsemen–the horse had stirrups all inlaid with gold and inlaid saddles, which are not to be found anywhere else in the world. The Javanese lords are so noble and exalted that there is no nation to compare with them over a wide area in these parts. They have their head shorn–half tonsured–as a mark of beauty, and they always run their hands over their hair from the forehead upwards unlike what European did. The lords of Java are revered like gods, with great respect and deep reverence.
>
> The lords go out hunting or pleasure-seeking in such exalted style. They spend all their time in pleasures, the retinues had so many lances in holders of gold and silver, so richly inlaid, with so many [harriers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harrier_(dog) "Harrier (dog)"), [greyhounds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greyhound "Greyhound") and other dogs; and they have so many pictures painted with images and hunting scenes. Their clothes are adorned with gold, their krises, swords, knives, cutlasses are all inlaid with gold; they have numbers of concubines, [jennets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jennet "Jennet"), elephants, oxen to draw the wagons of gilt and painted woodwork. The nobilities go out in triumphal cars, and if they go by sea they go in painted and ornamented kelulus; there are beautiful apartments for their women, other places for the nobles who accompanied him.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:3-2)[\[77\]](#refnote-:3):174–175,200

### Religion

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Majapahit&action=edit&section=22 "Edit section: Religion")\]

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1a/Candi_Jawi.jpg/250px-Candi_Jawi.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Candi_Jawi.jpg) [Jawi Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jawi_Temple "Jawi Temple"), a syncretic Hindu-Buddhist temple of Shiva-Buddha faith, dated from late 13th century Singhasari kingdom, which later renovated and preserved during the Majapahit period

[Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism "Buddhism"), [Shaivism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaivism "Shaivism"), and [Vaishnavism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaishnavism "Vaishnavism") were all practised: the king was regarded as the incarnation of the three. The _Nagarakretagama_ however, does not mention [Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam "Islam"), but there were certainly Muslim courtiers by this time.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ricklefs-8)[\[11\]](#refnote-ricklefs):19

[Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism "Hinduism") and [Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism "Buddhism") had already shaped the Javanese civilisation, religion, and spiritualism since the earlier era, from the 9th century [Mataram kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mataram_kingdom "Mataram kingdom"), Kahuripan, Kadiri, to Singhasari kingdom. It seems that both Hinduism and Buddhism were widely practised by Majapahit subjects. Nevertheless, native Javanese [shamanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shamanism "Shamanism") possibly still existed and was practised in peripheral rural regions.

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/28/COLLECTIE_TROPENMUSEUM_Gouden_reli%C3%ABf_met_de_voorstelling_van_Sutasoma_gedragen_door_Kalmasapada_TMnr_2960-319.jpg/250px-COLLECTIE_TROPENMUSEUM_Gouden_reli%C3%ABf_met_de_voorstelling_van_Sutasoma_gedragen_door_Kalmasapada_TMnr_2960-319.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:COLLECTIE_TROPENMUSEUM_Gouden_reli%C3%ABf_met_de_voorstelling_van_Sutasoma_gedragen_door_Kalmasapada_TMnr_2960-319.jpg) Gold figure from the Majapahit period representing [Sutasoma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sutasoma "Sutasoma") being borne by the man-eater [Kalmasapada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalmashapada "Kalmashapada")

The monarch and most of the royal family adhered to Hinduism, with certain emphasis on the cult of their own preferred main deity, either [Shiva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shivaism "Shivaism"), [Vishnu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaishnavism "Vaishnavism"), [Durga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durga "Durga") or other deities. The first king of Majapahit, [Kertarajasa Jayawardhana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raden_Wijaya "Raden Wijaya") was posthumously portrayed as [Harihara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harihara "Harihara"), the god combination of Shiva and Vishnu, in his mortuary temple in Candi Simping. [Mahayana Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahayana_Buddhism "Mahayana Buddhism") however, was also favoured by the royal family and officials of Majapahit. For example, the queen of Majapahit [Gayatri Rajapatni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gayatri_Rajapatni "Gayatri Rajapatni") and [Gajah Mada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gajah_Mada "Gajah Mada") are known as Buddhists.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Kompas.com-Gajah_Mada-0)[\[85\]](#refnote-Kompas.com-Gajah_Mada) Gayatri later posthumously portrayed as [Prajnaparamita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prajnaparamita "Prajnaparamita").

The state religion however, was possibly the _Siwa Buddha_, the Javanese [syncretism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syncretism "Syncretism") of Shaivism and Buddhism which emphasised the similarities between Shiva and Buddha, who were both described as [ascetics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ascetics "Ascetics") and spiritual teachers. The spiritual circumstances of Majapahit, seem to promote the harmony between the adherents of Shiva and Buddha. This is demonstrated in the manuscript [Sutasoma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sutasoma "Sutasoma") written in the 14th century by [Mpu Tantular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mpu_Tantular "Mpu Tantular") which promotes religious tolerance between Hinduism and Buddhism, specifically the syncretic Shiva-Buddha doctrine.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Siva-Buddha-0)[\[86\]](#refnote-Siva-Buddha)

During the Majapahit period, religious teaching played an important role in the society. The religious education could be done individually within households of [kshatriya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kshatriya "Kshatriya") nobles and [brahmana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brahmana "Brahmana") religious elites, or in a centre for religious teaching which resembles [ashram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashram "Ashram") or boarding school called _Mandala_ or _Kadewaguruan_. A _Kadewaguruan_ was usually located in remote areas far from habitation places, such as quiet forest, on hills or mountainous region. A _Kadewaguruan_ is led by a [mahāresi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maharishi "Maharishi") or high priest, who is also known as śiddharesi or dewaguru; hence the education centre is called _Kadewaguruan_.[&#8202;](#ref-back-102-0)[\[87\]](#refnote-102)

### Literature

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Majapahit&action=edit&section=23 "Edit section: Literature")\]

The literature of Majapahit was the continuation of [Javanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Javanese_literature "Javanese literature") [Kawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kawi_language "Kawi language") Hindu-Buddhist scholarly tradition that produces _[kakawin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kakawin "Kakawin")_ poem that has been developed in Java since the 9th century [Medang Mataram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medang_Kingdom "Medang Kingdom") era, all the way through [Kadiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kediri_Kingdom "Kediri Kingdom") and Singhasari periods. Notable Javanese literary works dated from an earlier period, such as Kadiri's 11th century Kanwa's [Arjunawiwaha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arjunawiwaha "Arjunawiwaha"), 12th-century Dharmaja's [Smaradahana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smaradahana "Smaradahana"), Sedah's [Bharatayuddha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bharatayuddha "Bharatayuddha"), Panuluh's [Hariwangsa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kakawin_Hariwangsa "Kakawin Hariwangsa") also popular [Panji cycles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panji_(prince) "Panji (prince)") are continuously preserved and rewritten by _Rakawi_ (Hindu-Buddhist poet or scholar) in Majapahit era. Notable literary works that were produced in the Majapahit period among others are Prapanca's [Nagarakretagama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nagarakretagama "Nagarakretagama"), Tantular's [Sutasoma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sutasoma "Sutasoma"), and [Tantu Pagelaran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tantu_Pagelaran "Tantu Pagelaran"). Popular tale of [Sri Tanjung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sri_Tanjung "Sri Tanjung") and [Damarwulan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Damarwulan "Damarwulan") also dated from Majapahit period. These [Old Javanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Javanese "Old Javanese") _kakawins_ were written and composed by _Rakawis_ (poets) to worship the king of the gods whose incarnation the king represented.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Siva-Buddha-1)[\[86\]](#refnote-Siva-Buddha)

Nagarakretagama composed by Prapanca in 1365 is a vital source of the premier historical account of Majapahit historiography. While Sutasoma is an important literature for modern Indonesian nationhood, since the national motto _[Bhinneka Tunggal Ika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhinneka_Tunggal_Ika "Bhinneka Tunggal Ika")_, which is usually translated as _[Unity in Diversity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unity_in_Diversity "Unity in Diversity")_, was taken from a _pupuh_ ( [canto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canto "Canto")) of this manuscript.[&#8202;](#ref-back-103-0)[\[88\]](#refnote-103)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/24/Palmleaf_of_Kakawin_Sutasoma_from_Java01.jpg/500px-Palmleaf_of_Kakawin_Sutasoma_from_Java01.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Palmleaf_of_Kakawin_Sutasoma_from_Java01.jpg) Palm leaf manuscript of _[Kakawin Sutasoma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kakawin_Sutasoma "Kakawin Sutasoma")_, a 14th-century Javanese poem

This quotation comes from Sutasoma canto 139, stanza 5. The full stanza reads as follows:

> _Rwâneka dhâtu winuwus Buddha Wiswa,_
>
> _Bhinnêki rakwa ring apan kena parwanosen,_
>
> _Mangka ng Jinatwa kalawan Siwatatwa tunggal,_
>
> _Bhinnêka tunggal ika tan hana dharma mangrwa._

Originally, the poem was meant to promote the religious tolerance between the Hindu and Buddhist religions, specifically promote the syncretic Shiva-Buddha doctrine.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Siva-Buddha-2)[\[86\]](#refnote-Siva-Buddha)

In _Yingya Shenglan_, Ma Huan describes the writing system used in Majapahit. For the writing, they had known the alphabet using _So-li_ (Chola–Coromandel/Southern India) letters. There is no paper or pen, they use _Chiao-chang_ ( _kajang_) or palm leaf ( _[lontar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palm-leaf_manuscript#Javanese_and_Balinese "Palm-leaf manuscript")_), written by scraping it with a sharp knife. They also have a developed language system and grammar.[&#8202;](#ref-back-yingyai-11)[\[26\]](#refnote-yingyai)

### Art

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Majapahit&action=edit&section=24 "Edit section: Art")\]

Further information: [Majapahit Terracotta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majapahit_Terracotta "Majapahit Terracotta")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1f/RA_3540013.JPG/250px-RA_3540013.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:RA_3540013.JPG) Bas reliefs of Tegowangi temple, dated from Majapahit period, demonstrate the East Javanese style.

The Pala school of art of the Indian [Pala Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pala_Empire "Pala Empire") influenced the art and architecture of Majapahit.[&#8202;](#ref-back-104-0)[\[89\]](#refnote-104)
Majapahit art was the continuation of East Javanese art, style and aesthetically developed since the 11th century during the Kediri and Singhasari period. Unlike the earlier naturalistic, relaxed and flowing figures of classical Central Java-style ( [Sailendra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sailendra "Sailendra") art c. 8th to 10th century), this East Javanese style demonstrates a stiffer pose, stylised and rendered in [wayang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayang "Wayang")-like figures, such as those carved on East Javanese temple's bas-reliefs. The bas-reliefs were projected rather flat from the background. This style was later preserved in [Balinese art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balinese_art "Balinese art"), especially in its [Kamasan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamasan "Kamasan") style classical paintings and Balinese wayang.

The statues of Hindu gods and Buddhist deities in Majapahit art were also the continuations of its previous Singhasari art. The statues of East Javanese period tends to be a stiffer and frontal-formal pose, compared to the statues of Central Javanese art (c. 9th century) that are more Indianized style, relaxed in [tribhanga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tribhanga "Tribhanga") pose. The stiffer pose of Majapahit gods statues is probably in accordance with the statue's function as the deified portrayal self of the dead Majapahit monarch. The carving, however, is richly decorated, especially with a fine floral carving of lotus plants carved on the stela behind the statue. Examples of Majapahit statues are the Harihara statue from Simping temple, believed to be the deified portrayal of [King Kertarajasa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raden_Wijaya "Raden Wijaya"), the statue of Parwati believed to be the portrayal of [Queen Tribhuwana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tribhuwana_Wijayatunggadewi "Tribhuwana Wijayatunggadewi"), and a statue of the queen [Suhita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suhita "Suhita") discovered at Jebuk, Kalangbret, Tulungagung, East Java.

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/40/Pair_of_door_guardians_SF_Asian_Art_Museum.JPG/250px-Pair_of_door_guardians_SF_Asian_Art_Museum.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Pair_of_door_guardians_SF_Asian_Art_Museum.JPG) Pair of door guardians from a temple, Eastern Java, 14th century, [Museum of Asian Art, San Francisco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Art_Museum_of_San_Francisco "Asian Art Museum of San Francisco")

Clay pottery and brick masonry are a popular feature in Majapahit art and architecture. The [Majapahit Terracotta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majapahit_Terracotta "Majapahit Terracotta") art also flourished in this period. Significant numbers of [terracotta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terracotta "Terracotta") artefacts were discovered in [Trowulan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trowulan "Trowulan"). The artefacts range from human and animal figurines, jars, vessels, water containers, [piggy banks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piggy_bank "Piggy bank"), bas reliefs, architectural ornaments, roof pinnacles, floor tiles, to pipes and roof tiles.

One of the most interesting findings is the Majapahit piggy bank. Several boar-shaped piggy banks have been discovered in Trowulan. It is probably the origin of Javanese-Indonesian word to refer for saving or money container. The word _celengan_ in [Javanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Javanese_language "Javanese language") and [Indonesian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_language "Indonesian language") means both "savings" and "piggy bank". It was derived from the word _celeng_ which means "wild boar", the [suffix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suffix "Suffix") "-an" was added to denote its likeness. One important specimen is stored in the National Museum of Indonesia, it has been reconstructed since this large piggy bank has been found broken to pieces.

Terracotta money boxes also have been found in different shapes, such as tubular or boxes, with slits to slip coins. Another important terracotta artefact is the head figurine of a man popularly thought to be the depiction of Gajah Mada, although it is not certain about who was depicted in these figurines.

### Architecture

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Majapahit&action=edit&section=25 "Edit section: Architecture")\]

Further information: [Candi of Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Candi_of_Indonesia "Candi of Indonesia")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/99/Close-up_of_KITLV_28318_-_Isidore_van_Kinsbergen_-_Relief_on_the_first_gallery_on_the_east_side_of_Panataran_near_Blitar_-_1867-02-1867-06_walls%2C_gate%2C_and_tower.jpg/250px-thumbnail.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Close-up_of_KITLV_28318_-_Isidore_van_Kinsbergen_-_Relief_on_the_first_gallery_on_the_east_side_of_Panataran_near_Blitar_-_1867-02-1867-06_walls,_gate,_and_tower.jpg)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e7/Close-up_of_KITLV_28291_-_Isidore_van_Kinsbergen_-_Relief_on_the_first_gallery_on_the_north_side_of_Panataran_near_Blitar_-_1867-02-1867-06_walls%2C_gate%2C_watchtower%2C_and_citizens.jpg/250px-thumbnail.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Close-up_of_KITLV_28291_-_Isidore_van_Kinsbergen_-_Relief_on_the_first_gallery_on_the_north_side_of_Panataran_near_Blitar_-_1867-02-1867-06_walls,_gate,_watchtower,_and_citizens.jpg)

Reliefs from the main temple of the [Panataran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panataran "Panataran") temple complex, depicting walls, gates, towers, and citizens

In his book _Yingya Shenglan_, Ma Huan also describes the Majapahit cities: most of them do not have walls surrounding the city or the suburbs. He describes the king's palace in Majapahit. The king's residence is surrounded with thick red brick walls more than three _[chang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhang_(unit) "Zhang (unit)")_ high (about 30feet 7inches or 9.32 metres), with length of more than 200 paces (340 yards or 310 metres) and on the wall there are two layers of gates, the palace is very well guarded and clean. The king's palace was a two-storey building, each of them 3 or 4 _chang_ high (9.32–12.42 metres or 30.6–40.7 feet). It had wooden plank floors and exposed mats made from rattan or reeds (presumably palm leaves), where people sat cross-legged. The roof was made of hardwood shingles (Javanese: _sirap_) laid as tiles.[&#8202;](#ref-back-yingyai-12)[\[26\]](#refnote-yingyai) This description of the palace is very different than that of Odoric of Pordenone, who visited Majapahit in the previous century during the reign of Jayanegara (1309–1328).[&#8202;](#ref-back-:19-1)[\[84\]](#refnote-:19):332 The reason of this was because Ma Huan likely stayed at a special area reserved for envoys, which was still 1.5 days' journey away from the real Majapahit palace.[&#8202;](#ref-back-kampungmaja-0)[\[90\]](#refnote-kampungmaja)

Odoric described the palace in more detail: The palace was described as being richer and finer than any existing palace at that time in the world. It had grand, broad, and lofty staircases; which the steps alternately made of gold and silver. The pavement of the palace was made alternately with one tile of gold and the other of silver, and the inside wall plated all over with gold, with knight sculptures of gold ornamented with precious stones. The ceiling of Majapahit palace was made of pure gold.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:1-2)[\[83\]](#refnote-:1):87

According to Ma Huan, the houses of commoners had thatched roofs ( [nipa palm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nipa_palm "Nipa palm") leaves). Every family has a storage shed made of bricks, about 3 or 4 _[chi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chi_(unit) "Chi (unit)")_ (48.9 inches or 124 centimetres) above the ground, where they kept the family property, and they lived on top of this building, to sit and sleep.[&#8202;](#ref-back-yingyai-13)[\[26\]](#refnote-yingyai) Not every house in Java looked like this: According to the _[History of the Song dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Song_(book) "History of Song (book)")_, houses in Java are grand and handsome–they are decorated with gold and jade. The chronicle also noted that when Chinese merchants arrive there they are received as guests in a public building.[&#8202;](#ref-back-notes_on_the_malay_archipelago-1)[\[60\]](#refnote-notes_on_the_malay_archipelago):16[&#8202;](#ref-back-106-0)[\[91\]](#refnote-106) This indicates that Ma Huan had not yet arrived at the center of Majapahit capital, and was only observing the outskirts.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTENugroho2011138-0)[\[92\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTENugroho2011138)[&#8202;](#ref-back-kampungmaja-1)[\[90\]](#refnote-kampungmaja)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/07/Three_reliefs_of_Candi_Tegowangi%2C_Dewi_Kunti_and_Batari_Durga_%28Sudamala_story%29.jpg/250px-Three_reliefs_of_Candi_Tegowangi%2C_Dewi_Kunti_and_Batari_Durga_%28Sudamala_story%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Three_reliefs_of_Candi_Tegowangi,_Dewi_Kunti_and_Batari_Durga_(Sudamala_story).jpg)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/28/Oemah_meten_op_het_erf_van_de_kantja_te_desa_Kajoe_Bihi_in_Bangli_op_Bali%2C_KITLV_8351.tiff/lossy-page1-330px-Oemah_meten_op_het_erf_van_de_kantja_te_desa_Kajoe_Bihi_in_Bangli_op_Bali%2C_KITLV_8351.tiff.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Oemah_meten_op_het_erf_van_de_kantja_te_desa_Kajoe_Bihi_in_Bangli_op_Bali,_KITLV_8351.tiff)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b9/Vrouwen_bezig_met_het_stampen_van_rijst_op_een_erf_van_een_woning_op_Bali%2C_KITLV_1400147.tiff/lossy-page1-250px-Vrouwen_bezig_met_het_stampen_van_rijst_op_een_erf_van_een_woning_op_Bali%2C_KITLV_1400147.tiff.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Vrouwen_bezig_met_het_stampen_van_rijst_op_een_erf_van_een_woning_op_Bali,_KITLV_1400147.tiff)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/60/COLLECTIE_TROPENMUSEUM_Straatgezicht_in_een_dorp_op_Bali_TMnr_10003533.jpg/250px-COLLECTIE_TROPENMUSEUM_Straatgezicht_in_een_dorp_op_Bali_TMnr_10003533.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:COLLECTIE_TROPENMUSEUM_Straatgezicht_in_een_dorp_op_Bali_TMnr_10003533.jpg)

Tegowangi temple reliefs and traditional houses of Bali. Balinese architecture is deeply influence by Majapahit.

The Majapahit temple architecture follows the east Javanese styles, in contrast to earlier central Javanese style. This east Javanese temple style is also dated back from Kediri period c. 11th century. The shapes of Majapahit temples tend to be slender and tall, with a roof constructed from multiple parts of stepped sections formed a combined roof structure curved upward smoothly creating the [perspective illusion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perspective_(visual) "Perspective (visual)") that the temple is perceived taller than its actual height. The pinnacle of the temples are usually cube (mostly Hindu temples), sometimes dagoba cylindrical structures (Buddhist temples). Although some of the temples dated from Majapahit period used [andesite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andesite "Andesite") or [sandstone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandstone "Sandstone"), the red [bricks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brick "Brick") is also a popular construction material.

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fb/Bajang_Ratu_Gate_Trowulan.jpg/120px-Bajang_Ratu_Gate_Trowulan.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Bajang_Ratu_Gate_Trowulan.jpg)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7f/Candi_Jabung_B.JPG/120px-Candi_Jabung_B.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Candi_Jabung_B.JPG)

**Left to right**:

- The 16.5-metre tall Bajang Ratu [Paduraksa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paduraksa "Paduraksa") gate, at Trowulan, echoed the grandeur of Majapahit.
- [Jabung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jabung "Jabung") temple near Paiton, Probolinggo, East Java, dated from Majapahit period

Although [brick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brick "Brick") had been used in the _[candi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Candi_of_Indonesia "Candi of Indonesia")_ of Indonesia's classical age, it was Majapahit architects of the 14th and 15th centuries who mastered it.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Schoppert1997-0)[\[93\]](#refnote-Schoppert1997) Making use of a vine sap and [palm sugar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palm_sugar "Palm sugar") mortar, their temples had a strong geometric quality. The example of Majapahit temples are Brahu temple in Trowulan, [Pari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pari_Temple "Pari Temple") in Sidoarjo, [Jabung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jabung "Jabung") in Probolinggo, and [Surawana temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Candi_Surawana "Candi Surawana") near Kediri. Jabung temple was mentioned in Nagarakretagama as Bajrajinaparamitapura, despite some parts of its roof and pinnacles are now missing, it is one of the most well-preserved Majapahit temple architecture. Another example includes Gunung Gangsir temple near Pasuruan. Some of the temples are dated from the earlier period but renovated and expanded during Majapahit era, such as [Penataran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Candi_Penataran "Candi Penataran"), the largest temple in East Java dated back to [Kediri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kediri_(historical_kingdom) "Kediri (historical kingdom)") era. This temple was identified in Nagarakretagama as Palah temple and reported being visited by King Hayam Wuruk during his royal tour across East Java. Another notable temple of Eastern Javanese style is [Jawi temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jawi_temple "Jawi temple") in Pandaan–also visited by King Hayam Wuruk, the temple was mentioned in Nagarakretagama as Jajawa, and was dedicated as a mortuary temple for his great-grandfather, King Kertanegara of Singhasari.

Some of the typical architectural styles are believed to be developed during Majapahit era; such as tall and slender roofed red brick gate commonly called as _kori agung_ or _[paduraksa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paduraksa "Paduraksa")_, and also split gate of _[candi bentar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Candi_bentar "Candi bentar")_. The large split gate of Wringin Lawang located at Jatipasar, Trowulan, Mojokerto, East Java, is one of the oldest and the largest surviving _candi bentar_ dated from Majapahit era. The _candi bentar_ took shape of typical Majapahit temple structure–consists of three parts; foot, body and tall roof–evenly split into two mirroring structures to make a passage in the centre for people to walk through. This type of split gate has no doors and provides no real defensive purpose but narrowing the passage. It probably only served the ceremonial and aesthetic purpose, to create the sense of grandeur, before entering the next compound through tall roof _paduraksa_ gate with enclosed door. The example of _kori agung_ or _paduraksa_ style gate is the elegant Bajang Ratu gate richly decorated with Kala demon, cyclops and also the bas-relief telling the story of Sri Tanjung. Those typical Majapahit architectural style has deeply influenced the Javanese and [Balinese architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balinese_architecture "Balinese architecture") of later period. The current prevalence of Majapahit style pendopo pavilion, candi bentar and paduraksa gates was owed to the influence of Majapahit aesthetics on Javanese and Balinese architecture.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Made_Wijaya-0)[\[94\]](#refnote-Made_Wijaya)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e2/Looking_down_from_the_top_of_Cetho_Temple%2C_2016-10-13.jpg/250px-Looking_down_from_the_top_of_Cetho_Temple%2C_2016-10-13.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Looking_down_from_the_top_of_Cetho_Temple,_2016-10-13.jpg) The stepped terraces, pavilions, and [split gates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Candi_bentar "Candi bentar") of [Cetho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ceto_Temple "Ceto Temple") temple complex on mount Lawu slopes

In later period near the fall of Majapahit, the art and architecture of Majapahit witnessed the revival of indigenous native Austronesian [megalithic architectural elements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megalithic_architectural_elements "Megalithic architectural elements"), such as [Sukuh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Candi_Sukuh "Candi Sukuh") and [Cetho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Candi_Cetho "Candi Cetho") temples on western slopes of [Mount Lawu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Lawu "Mount Lawu"). Unlike previous Majapahit temples that demonstrate typical Hindu architecture of high-rise towering structure, the shape of these temples are [step pyramid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Step_pyramid "Step pyramid"), quite similar to Mesoamerican [pyramids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pyramid "Pyramid"). The stepped pyramid structure called _Punden Berundak_ (stepped mounds) is a common megalithic structure during [Indonesian prehistoric era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prehistoric_Indonesia "Prehistoric Indonesia") before the adoption of Hindu-Buddhist culture.

## Economy

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Majapahit&action=edit&section=26 "Edit section: Economy")\]

The _[Daoyi Zhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daoyi_Zhil%C3%BCe "Daoyi Zhilüe")_, which was written c. 1339 CE, mentioned about Java's wealth and prosperity of the period:

> "The fields of Java are rich and its soil is level and well watered, therefore grain and rice are abundant, twice as much as in other countries. The people do not steal, and what is dropped on the road is not taken up. The common saying: "prosperous Java" means this country. Men and women wrap up their head and wear long clothes."[&#8202;](#ref-back-110-0)[\[95\]](#refnote-110):124

Also in _Yingya Shenglan_, Ma Huan reported the Javanese economy and market. [Rice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rice "Rice") is harvested twice a year, and its grain is small. They also harvest white [sesame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sesame "Sesame") and [lentils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lentil "Lentil"), but there is no [wheat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wheat "Wheat"). This land produces [sapan wood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sapan_Wood "Sapan Wood") (useful to produce red dye), [diamonds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diamond "Diamond"), [sandalwood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandalwood "Sandalwood"), [incense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Incense "Incense"), _puyang_ pepper, [cantharides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantharides "Cantharides") (green beetles used for medicine), [steel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steel "Steel"), [turtles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turtle "Turtle"), tortoiseshell, strange and rare birds; such as a large [parrot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parrot "Parrot") as big as a hen, red and green parrots, five-colored parrots, (all of them can imitate the human voice), also [guinea fowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guinea_fowl "Guinea fowl"), ' bird hanging upside down ', five-coloured pigeon, [peacock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peacock "Peacock"), 'betel tree bird', pearl bird, and green pigeons. The beasts here are strange: there are white deer, white [monkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monkey "Monkey"), and various other animals. Pigs, [goats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goat "Goat"), cattle, [horses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horse "Horse"), poultries, and there are all types of [ducks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duck "Duck"), however donkeys and geese are not found.[&#8202;](#ref-back-yingyai-14)[\[26\]](#refnote-yingyai)

For the fruits, there are all kinds of [bananas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banana "Banana"), [coconut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coconut "Coconut"), [sugarcane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugarcane "Sugarcane"), [pomegranate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pomegranate "Pomegranate"), [lotus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lotus_(plant) "Lotus (plant)"), _mang-chi-shi_ ( _manggis_ or [mangosteen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mangosteen "Mangosteen")), [watermelon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watermelon "Watermelon") and _lang Ch'a_ ( _langsat_ or [lanzones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lanzones "Lanzones")). _Mang-chi-shi_–is something like a pomegranate, peel it like an orange, it has four lumps of white flesh, sweet and sour taste and very delicious. _Lang-ch'a_ is a fruit similar to Loquat, but larger contained three blocky white flesh with a sweet and sour taste. Sugarcane has white stems, large and coarse, with roots reaching 3 _chang_ (30 feet 7 inches). In addition, all types of squash and vegetables are there, just a shortage of [peach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peach "Peach"), [plum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plum "Plum") and [leek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leek "Leek").[&#8202;](#ref-back-yingyai-15)[\[26\]](#refnote-yingyai)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/04/Majapahit%2C_Piggy_Bank.jpg/250px-Majapahit%2C_Piggy_Bank.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Majapahit,_Piggy_Bank.jpg) Majapahit [terracotta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terracotta "Terracotta") [piggy bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piggy_bank "Piggy bank"), 14th or 15th century [Trowulan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trowulan "Trowulan"), [East Java](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Java "East Java") (collection of [National Museum of Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_Indonesia "National Museum of Indonesia"), [Jakarta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jakarta "Jakarta"))

Taxes and fines were paid in cash. Javanese economy had been partly monetised since the late 8th century, using gold and silver coins. Previously, the 9th-century [Wonoboyo hoard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wonoboyo_hoard "Wonoboyo hoard") discovered in Central Java shows that ancient Javan gold coins were seed-shaped, similar to corn, while the silver coins were similar to buttons. In about the year 1300, in the reign of Majapahit's first king, an important change took place: the indigenous coinage was completely replaced by imported Chinese copper cash. About 10,388 ancient Chinese coins weighing about 40kg were even unearthed from the backyard of a local commoner in [Sidoarjo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sidoarjo "Sidoarjo") in November 2008. Indonesian Ancient Relics Conservation Bureau (BP3) of East Java verified that those coins dated as early as Majapahit era.[&#8202;](#ref-back-111-0)[\[96\]](#refnote-111) The reason for using the foreign currency is not given in any source, but most scholars assume it was due to the increasing complexity of Javanese economy and a desire for a [currency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Currency "Currency") system that used much smaller denominations suitable for use in everyday market transactions. This was a role for which gold and silver are not well suited.[&#8202;](#ref-back-miksic-2)[\[54\]](#refnote-miksic):107 These _kepeng_ Chinese coins were thin rounded copper coins with a square hole in the centre of it. The hole was meant to tie together the money in a string of coins. These small changes–the imported Chinese copper coins–enabled Majapahit further invention, a method of savings by using a slit earthenware coin container. These are commonly found in Majapahit ruins, the slit in the small opening to put the coins in. The most popular shape is boar-shaped _celengan_ (piggy bank).

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/Trowulan_ancient_canal_1.JPG/250px-Trowulan_ancient_canal_1.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Trowulan_ancient_canal_1.JPG) Ancient red-brick canal discovered in Trowulan. Majapahit had a well-developed irrigation infrastructure.

Some idea of the scale of the internal economy can be gathered from scattered data in inscriptions. The Canggu inscriptions dated 1358 mentions 78 ferry crossings in the country (mandala Java).[&#8202;](#ref-back-miksic-3)[\[54\]](#refnote-miksic):107 Majapahit inscriptions mention a large number of occupational specialities, ranging from gold and silversmiths to drink vendors and butchers. Although many of these occupations had existed in earlier times, the proportion of the population earning an income from non-agrarian pursuits seems to have become even greater during the Majapahit era.

The great prosperity of Majapahit was probably due to two factors. Firstly, the northeast lowlands of Java were suitable for rice [cultivation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tillage "Tillage"), and during Majapahit's prime numerous [irrigation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irrigation "Irrigation") projects were undertaken, some with government assistance. Secondly, Majapahit's ports on the north coast were probably significant stations along the route to obtain the spices of [Maluku](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maluku_Islands "Maluku Islands"), and as the spices passed through Java they would have provided an important source of income for Majapahit.[&#8202;](#ref-back-miksic-4)[\[54\]](#refnote-miksic):107

The Nagarakretagama states that the fame of the ruler of Wilwatikta (a synonym for Majapahit) attracted foreign merchants from far and wide, including Indians, Khmers, Siamese, and Chinese among others. While in the later period, _Yingya Shenglan_ mentioned that large numbers of Chinese traders and Muslim merchants from the west (from Arabia and India, but mostly from Muslim states in Sumatra and the Malay Peninsula) are settling in Majapahit port cities, such as Tuban, Gresik and Hujung Galuh ( [Surabaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surabaya "Surabaya")). A special tax was levied against some foreigners, possibly those who had taken up semi-permanent residence in Java and conducted some type of enterprise other than foreign trade. The Majapahit Empire had trading links with Chinese [Ming dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ming_dynasty "Ming dynasty"), [Đại Việt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%90%E1%BA%A1i_Vi%E1%BB%87t "Đại Việt") and [Champa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Champa "Champa") in today Vietnam, [Cambodia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambodia "Cambodia"), Siamese [Ayutthayan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayutthaya_Kingdom "Ayutthaya Kingdom"), Burmese [Martaban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martaban "Martaban") and the south Indian [Vijayanagara Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vijayanagara_Empire "Vijayanagara Empire").[&#8202;](#ref-back-112-0)[\[97\]](#refnote-112)

During the Majapahit era, almost all of the commodities from Asia were found in Java. This is because of extensive shipping by the Majapahit empire using various type of ships, particularly the jong, for trading to faraway places.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTENugroho201156–60,_286–291-0)[\[98\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTENugroho201156–60,_286–291) Ma Huan (Zheng He's translator) who visited Java in 1413, stated that ports in Java were trading goods and offer services that were more numerous and more complete than other ports in Southeast Asia.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTENugroho2011233–234,_239–240-0)[\[99\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTENugroho2011233–234,_239–240)

## Administration

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Majapahit&action=edit&section=27 "Edit section: Administration")\]

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/97/Surya_Majapahit.jpg/250px-Surya_Majapahit.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Surya_Majapahit.jpg)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fe/Surya_Majapahit_2.jpg/250px-Surya_Majapahit_2.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Surya_Majapahit_2.jpg)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2f/Candi_Ceto_Majapahit_Sun%2C_Surakarta_1264.jpg/250px-Candi_Ceto_Majapahit_Sun%2C_Surakarta_1264.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Candi_Ceto_Majapahit_Sun,_Surakarta_1264.jpg)

Three designs of [Surya Majapahit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surya_Majapahit "Surya Majapahit"), the sun emblem of the kingdom

During the reign of [Hayam Wuruk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hayam_Wuruk "Hayam Wuruk"), Majapahit employed a well-organised bureaucratic structure for administrative purposes. The hierarchy and structure relatively remain intact and unchanged throughout Majapahit history.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SNI451-0)[\[100\]](#refnote-SNI451) The king is the paramount ruler, as the [chakravartin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chakravartin "Chakravartin") he is considered the universal ruler and believed to be the living god on earth. The king holds the highest political authority and legitimacy.

### Law and order

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Majapahit&action=edit&section=28 "Edit section: Law and order")\]

The [law and order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_and_order_(politics) "Law and order (politics)") of Majapahit kingdom follows the regulations related to the criminal code of law which became the guidelines for people's lives at that time. This rule of law can be found in a manuscript called _[Kutaramanawa Dharmashastra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kutaramanawa_Dharmashastra?action=edit&redlink=1 "Kutaramanawa Dharmashastra (page does not exist)")_ or translated as "the Book of Religious Legislation". According to the historian Slamet Muljana's notes in the book _Perundang-undangan Madjapahit_ (1967), there is no definite record regarding the time when this criminal law was drafted. However, based on some evidence, it can be concluded that this book of law written in old Javanese was originated from the Majapahit period during the reign of King [Rajasanagara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hayam_Wuruk "Hayam Wuruk").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Hukum_Majapahit-0)[\[101\]](#refnote-Hukum_Majapahit)

In Majapahit, the punishment for the [crime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crime "Crime") of [murder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murder "Murder") was death. According to the manuscript Kidung Sorandaka, it was said that Demung Sora, a minister of Majapahit court, was sentenced to death penalty under the _Astadusta_ provisions for his crime on killing Mahisa Anabrang. _Astadusta_ is a part of the _Kutaramanawa Dharmashastra_ which regulates the punishment for murder.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Hukum_Majapahit-1)[\[101\]](#refnote-Hukum_Majapahit) Overall, this criminal code consists of 19 sections that regulate various aspects of life. In addition to the punishment for the crime of murders which are summarised in the _Astadusta_ section, there are also rules regarding [buying and selling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trade "Trade"), [debts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Debts "Debts"), marriage, and [pawnbroker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pawnbroker "Pawnbroker"). The punishments handed down included the death penalty, mutilation of the guilty body parts, fines and compensation. Additional penalties included ransom, confiscation, and money compensation for medication and drugs.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Hukum_Majapahit-2)[\[101\]](#refnote-Hukum_Majapahit)

According to the Chinese description in _[Yingya Shenglan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yingya_Shenglan "Yingya Shenglan")_, when the one being stabbed was wounded and dead, the murderer will flee and hide for three days, then he will not lose his life. But if he was caught during the fight, he will instantly be stabbed to death (execution by stabbing). The country of Majapahit knows no [caning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caning "Caning") for major or minor punishment. They tied the guilty men on his hands in the back with rattan rope and paraded them, and then stabbed the offender in the back where there is a floating rib which resulted in instant death.[&#8202;](#ref-back-yingyai-16)[\[26\]](#refnote-yingyai) Judicial executions of this kind were frequent.

### Bureaucracy officials

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Majapahit&action=edit&section=29 "Edit section: Bureaucracy officials")\]

During his daily administration, the king is assisted by bureaucratic state officials that also included the close relatives of the kings that hold certain esteemed titles. The royal order or edict usually transmitted from the king to the high officials well to their subordinates. The officials in Majapahit courts are:

- _Rakryan Mahamantri Katrini_, usually reserved for the king's heir
- _Rakryan Mantri ri Pakira-kiran_, the board of [ministers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_(government) "Minister (government)") that conduct the daily administration
- _Dharmmadhyaksa_, the officials of laws, state laws as well as religious laws
- _Dharmma-upapatti_, the officials concerning religious affairs

Within the ministers of _Rakryan Mantri ri Pakira-kiran_ there is the most important and the highest minister titled _Rakryan Mapatih_ or _Patih Hamangkubhumi_. This position is analogous to [prime minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_minister "Prime minister"), and together with the king, they determine the important state policies, including war or peace. Among the Dharmmadhyaksa officials, there is _Dharmmadhyaksa ring Kasewan_ (State's highest Hindu [Shivaist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shivaist "Shivaist") priest) and _Dharmmadhyaksa ring Kasogatan_ (State's highest Buddhist priest), both are the authorities of the religious law of each dharmic faiths.

There is also the board of advisors which consists of the elders within the royal family called _Bhattara Saptaprabhu_. This council consists of seven influential elders–mostly directly related to the king. They are the _Bhres_ (Duke or Duchess) acted as regional kings, the rulers of Majapahit provinces. This council congregates, offers advice, considered the king, and often formed an assembly to judge a certain important case in the court. Example of their office was, their sentence to temporarily suspend Mahamantri Gajah Mada, as a punishment since he was held responsible for the shamefully disastrous [Bubat incident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Bubat "Battle of Bubat"). The council also sentenced the execution of Raden Gajah (Narapati) for decapitating Bhre Wirabhumi in [Regreg war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regreg_War "Regreg War").[&#8202;](#ref-back-BP-Sejarah_Nasional-0)[\[102\]](#refnote-BP-Sejarah_Nasional):481

### Territorial hierarchy

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Majapahit&action=edit&section=30 "Edit section: Territorial hierarchy")\]

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a3/Majapahit_Core_and_Provinces.svg/500px-Majapahit_Core_and_Provinces.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Majapahit_Core_and_Provinces.svg) Majapahit core realm and provinces ( _Mancanagara_) in eastern and central parts of Java, including islands of Madura and Bali

Majapahit recognise the hierarchy classifications of lands within its realm:

1. _[Bhumi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bh%C5%ABmi "Bhūmi")_: the kingdom, ruled by the king
2. _Nagara_: the province, ruled by the _rajya_ (governor), or _natha_ (lord), or _bhre_ (prince or duke)
3. _Watek_: the regency, administered by _wiyasa_,
4. _Kuwu_: the district, administered by _akuwu_,
5. _Wanua_: the village, administered by _thani_,
6. _Kabuyutan_: the hamlet or sanctuary place.

During its formation, Majapahit traditional realm only consists of lesser vassal kingdoms (provinces) in eastern and central Java. This region is ruled by provincial kings called _Paduka Bhattara_ with the title _Bhre_, shortened form of _Bhatara i_ (Bhatara of). This title is the highest position below the monarch and similar to duke or duchess. Usually, this position reserved for the close relatives of the king. They have to administer their provinces, collect taxes, send annual tributes to the capital, and manage the defences of their borders.

During the reign of [Dyah Hayam Wuruk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hayam_Wuruk "Hayam Wuruk") (formally King Rajasanagara; 1350–1389) there were 12 provinces of Majapahit, administered by king's close relatives:

| Provinces | Titles | Rulers | Relation to the King |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| [Kahuripan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kahuripan "Kahuripan") (or [Janggala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Janggala "Janggala"), today [Sidoarjo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sidoarjo_Regency "Sidoarjo Regency")) | Bhre Kahuripan | Tribhuwanatunggadewi | queen mother |
| Daha (former capital of [Kediri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kediri_(historical_kingdom) "Kediri (historical kingdom)")) | Bhre Daha | Rajadewi Maharajasa | aunt and also mother-in-law |
| Tumapel (former capital of [Singhasari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singhasari "Singhasari")) | Bhre Tumapel | Kertawardhana | father |
| Wengker (today [Ponorogo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ponorogo "Ponorogo")) | Bhre Wengker | Wijayarajasa | uncle and also father-in-law |
| Matahun (today West [Bojonegoro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bojonegoro "Bojonegoro")) | Bhre Matahun | Rajasawardhana | husband of the duchess of Lasem, king's cousin |
| Wirabhumi ( [Blambangan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blambangan "Blambangan")) | Bhre Wirabhumi | Bhre Wirabhumi[&#8202;](#ref-back-118-0)[\[note 16\]](#refnote-118) | son |
| Paguhan (today upstream of [Serayu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serayu_River "Serayu River")) | Bhre Paguhan | Singhawardhana | brother in-law |
| Kabalan (today East [Bojonegoro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bojonegoro "Bojonegoro")) | Bhre Kabalan | Kusumawardhani[&#8202;](#ref-back-jointheirs-0)[\[note 17\]](#refnote-jointheirs) | daughter |
| Pawanuan | Bhre Pawanuan | Surawardhani | niece |
| Lasem (a coastal town in [Central Java](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Java "Central Java")) | Bhre Lasem | Rajasaduhita Indudewi | cousin |
| [Pajang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pajang "Pajang") (today [Surakarta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surakarta "Surakarta")) | Bhre Pajang | Rajasaduhita Iswari | sister |
| [Mataram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mataram_Kingdom "Mataram Kingdom") (today [Yogyakarta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yogyakarta "Yogyakarta")) | Bhre Mataram | Wikramawardhana[&#8202;](#ref-back-jointheirs-1)[\[note 17\]](#refnote-jointheirs) | nephew and son-in-law |

[&#8202;](#ref-back-120-0)[\[103\]](#refnote-120) List of Majapahit provinces administered by the king's relatives based on [Waringin Pitu inscription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waringin_Pitu_inscription?action=edit&redlink=1 "Waringin Pitu inscription (page does not exist)") (1447) written during the reign of Dyah Kertawijaya (formally King Wijayaparakramawardhana).

| Provinces | Titles | Rulers | Relation to the King |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| [Kahuripan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kahuripan "Kahuripan") (or [Janggala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Janggala "Janggala"), today [Sidoarjo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sidoarjo_Regency "Sidoarjo Regency")) | _Bhre_ Kahuripan | Rājasawardhana Dyah Wijayakumāra | son |
| Daha (former capital of [Kediri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kediri_(historical_kingdom) "Kediri (historical kingdom)")) | _Bhre_ Daha | Jayawardhanī Dyah Jayéswarī | wife |
| Tumapel (former capital of [Singhasari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singhasari "Singhasari")) | _Bhre_ Tumapel | Singhawikramawardhana Dyah Suraprabhāwa | son |
| Wengker (today [Ponorogo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ponorogo "Ponorogo")) | _Bhre_ Wengker | Girīsawardhana Dyah Sūryawikrama | son |
| Matahun (today West [Bojonegoro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bojonegoro "Bojonegoro")) | _Bhre_ Matahun | Wijayaparākrama Dyah Samarawijaya | grandson |
| Wirabhumi ( [Blambangan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blambangan "Blambangan")) | _Bhre_ Wirabhumi | Rājasawardhanendudewī Dyah Puréswari | granddaughter-in-law |
| Jagaraga | _Bhre_ Jagaraga | Wijayendudewī Dyah Wijayaduhitā | niece |
| Tanjungpura | _Bhre_ Tanjungpura | Manggalawardhanī Dyah Suraghārinī | daughter-in-law |
| Kabalan (today East [Bojonegoro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bojonegoro "Bojonegoro")) | _Bhre_ Kabalan | Mahāmisī Dyah Sāwitrī | daughter-in-law |
| Kembang Jenar | _Bhre_ Kembang Jenar | Rājānandanéswarī Dyah Sudharminī | niece-in-law |
| Singhapura | _Bhre_ Singhapura | Rājāsawardhanadewī Dyah Srīpurā | daughter-in-law |
| [Pajang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pajang "Pajang") (today [Surakarta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surakarta "Surakarta")) | _Bhre_ Pajang | Dyah Suréswari | niece |
| Keling | _Bhre_ Keling | Girindrawardhana Dyah Wijayakarana | grandson |
| Kalinggapura | _Bhre_ Kalinggapura | Kalamawarnadewī Dyah Sudāyitā | granddaughter-in-law |

### Territorial division

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Majapahit&action=edit&section=31 "Edit section: Territorial division")\]

Main article: [Territories of Majapahit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territories_of_Majapahit "Territories of Majapahit")

When Majapahit entered the thalassocracy imperial phase during the administration of Gajah Mada, several overseas vassal states were included within the Majapahit sphere of influence, as a result, the new larger territorial concept was defined:

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/71/Majapahit_Empire.svg/500px-Majapahit_Empire.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Majapahit_Empire.svg) The extent of Majapahit's influence under Hayam Wuruk in 1365 according to Nagarakretagama

- _**Negara Agung**_, or the Grand State, the core kingdom. The traditional or initial area of Majapahit during its formation before entering the imperial phase. This includes the capital city and the surrounding areas where the king effectively exercises his government. The area in and around royal capital of [Trowulan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trowulan "Trowulan"), the port of Canggu and sections of [Brantas River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brantas_River "Brantas River") valley near the capital, also mountainous areas south and southeast of the capital, all the way to [Pananggungan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Penanggungan "Mount Penanggungan") and [Arjuno-Welirang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arjuno-Welirang "Arjuno-Welirang") peaks, are the core realm of the kingdom. The Brantas river valley corridor, connecting Majapahit Trowulan area to Canggu and estuarine areas in Kahuripan (Sidoarjo) and [Hujung Galuh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Janggala "Janggala") port (Surabaya) are considered parts of _Negara Agung_.
- _**Mancanegara**_, areas surrounding _Negara Agung_–traditionally refer to Majapahit provinces in East and Central Java. This area covered the eastern half of Java, with all its provinces ruled by the _Bhres_ (dukes), the king's close relatives. These areas are directly influenced by Javanese Majapahit court culture and obliged to pay annual tributes. These areas usually possess their rulers that might be directly related, foster an alliance or intermarried with the Majapahit royal family. Majapahit stationed their officials and officers in these places and regulate their foreign trade activities and collect taxes, yet they enjoyed substantial internal autonomy. This includes the rest of [Java](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Java "Java") island, [Madura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madura "Madura") and [Bali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bali "Bali"). However, in the later period, overseas provinces which have developed culture reflected or comparable to those of Java, or possess significant trading importance, are also considered _mancanegara_. Either the province has its own native rulers subject (vassal) to the king, or a regent appointed and sent by the king to rule the region. This realm includes [Dharmasraya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melayu_Kingdom "Melayu Kingdom"), [Pagaruyung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pagaruyung_Kingdom "Pagaruyung Kingdom"), [Lampung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lampung "Lampung") and [Palembang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palembang "Palembang") in [Sumatra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sumatra "Sumatra").
- _**Nusantara**_, areas which do not reflect Javanese culture, but are included as colonies and they had to pay an annual tribute. They had their native polity intact, enjoyed substantial autonomy and internal freedom, and Majapahit did not necessarily station their officials or military officers here; however, any challenges on Majapahit oversight might draw a severe response. These areas such as the vassal kingdoms and colonies in the [Malay Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_Peninsula "Malay Peninsula"), [Borneo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borneo "Borneo"), [Lesser Sunda Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lesser_Sunda_Islands "Lesser Sunda Islands"), [Sulawesi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sulawesi "Sulawesi") and [Maluku](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maluku_Islands "Maluku Islands").

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7d/Filippine%2C_provincia_di_agusan%2C_immagine_hindu%2C_statuetta_in_oro_massiccio%2C_xiii_secolo.jpg/250px-Filippine%2C_provincia_di_agusan%2C_immagine_hindu%2C_statuetta_in_oro_massiccio%2C_xiii_secolo.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Filippine,_provincia_di_agusan,_immagine_hindu,_statuetta_in_oro_massiccio,_xiii_secolo.jpg) A 1.79 kilogram, 21-karat Majapahit period [gold image](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Tara "Golden Tara") discovered in [Agusan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Esperanza,_Agusan "Esperanza, Agusan"), [Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippines "Philippines"), copied Nganjuk bronze images of the early Majapahit period,[&#8202;](#ref-back-121-0)[\[104\]](#refnote-121) signify Majapahit cultural influence on southern Philippines.

[Nagarakretagama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nagarakretagama#Descriptions_of_the_Majapahit_realm "Nagarakretagama") mentioned more than 80 places in the archipelago described as the vassal states.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Britannica-Majapahit-2)[\[10\]](#refnote-Britannica-Majapahit) In Canto 13, several lands on [Sumatra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sumatra "Sumatra") are mentioned, and some possibly correspond to contemporary areas: [Jambi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jambi "Jambi"), [Palembang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palembang "Palembang"), Teba (either [Tebo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tebo_Regency "Tebo Regency") in Jambi or [Toba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toba_Samosir_Regency "Toba Samosir Regency") areas by [Lake Toba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Toba "Lake Toba")), and [Dharmasraya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dharmasraya "Dharmasraya"). Also mentioned are Kandis, Kahwas, [Minangkabau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minangkabau_people "Minangkabau people"), [Siak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siak_Regency "Siak Regency"), [Rokan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rokan_Hilir_Regency "Rokan Hilir Regency"), [Kampar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kampar_Regency "Kampar Regency") and [Pane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pannai "Pannai"), Kampe, [Haru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aru_Kingdom "Aru Kingdom") (coastal [North Sumatra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Sumatra "North Sumatra"), today around [Medan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medan "Medan")) and [Mandailing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandailing "Mandailing"). Tamiyang ( [Aceh Tamiang Regency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aceh_Tamiang_Regency "Aceh Tamiang Regency")), negara Perlak ( [Peureulak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peureulak "Peureulak")) and [Padang Lawas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Padang_Lawas_Regency "Padang Lawas Regency"), are noted in the west, together with Samudra ( [Samudra Pasai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samudra_Pasai "Samudra Pasai")) and [Lamuri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lamuri "Lamuri"), Batan ( [Bintan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bintan "Bintan")), [Lampung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lampung "Lampung"), and [Barus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barus "Barus"). Also listed are the states of Tanjungnegara (believed to be on [Borneo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borneo "Borneo")): [Kapuas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kapuas_River "Kapuas River") [Katingan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katingan_Regency "Katingan Regency"), [Sampit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sampit "Sampit"), Kota Lingga, [Kotawaringin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kotawaringin "Kotawaringin"), [Sambas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sambas_Regency "Sambas Regency"), and Lawas.

In Nagarakretagama Canto 14 more lands are noted: Kadandangan, [Landa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landak_Regency "Landak Regency"), Samadang, Tirem, Sedu ( [Sibu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sibu "Sibu") in [Sarawak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarawak "Sarawak")), Barune ( [Brunei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brunei "Brunei")), Kalka, Saludung (Serudong River in Sabah[&#8202;](#ref-back-122-0)[\[105\]](#refnote-122)), Solot ( [Sulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sulu_Province "Sulu Province")), Pasir, [Barito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barito_River "Barito River"), Sawaku, [Tabalung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tabalong_Regency "Tabalong Regency"), and [Tanjung Kutei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kutai "Kutai"). In Hujung Medini ( [Malay Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_Peninsula "Malay Peninsula")), [Pahang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pahang "Pahang") is mentioned first. Next [Langkasuka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Langkasuka "Langkasuka"), Saimwang, [Kelantan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kelantan "Kelantan") and [Trengganu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trengganu "Trengganu"), [Johor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johor "Johor"), [Paka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paka,_Malaysia "Paka, Malaysia"), [Muar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muar_town "Muar town"), [Dungun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dungun "Dungun"), Tumasik ( [Temasek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temasek "Temasek"), where [Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore "Singapore") is today), Kelang ( [Klang Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Klang_Valley "Klang Valley")) and [Kedah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kedah "Kedah"), Jerai ( [Gunung Jerai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gunung_Jerai "Gunung Jerai")), Kanjapiniran, all are united.

Also in Canto 14 are territories east of Java: [Badahulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bedulu "Bedulu") and [Lo Gajah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goa_Gajah "Goa Gajah") (part of today's [Bali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bali "Bali")). Gurun and [Sukun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nusa_Penida "Nusa Penida"), [Taliwang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taliwang "Taliwang"), Sapi (Sape town, east end of Sumbawa island, by the [Sape Strait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sape_Strait "Sape Strait")) and [Dompo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dompu "Dompu"), [Sang Hyang Api](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sangeang_Api "Sangeang Api"), [Bima](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bima "Bima"). [Sheran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seram_Island "Seram Island") and Hutan Kadali ( [Buru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buru "Buru") island). [Gurun island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gorong_archipelago "Gorong archipelago"), and [Lombok Merah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lombok "Lombok"). Together with prosperous [Sasak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sasak_people "Sasak people") (central, north and east [Lombok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lombok "Lombok")) are already ruled. [Bantayan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bantayan_Island "Bantayan Island") with [Luwu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luwu_Regency "Luwu Regency").
