Further east are Udamakatraya ( [Sangir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sangihe_Islands "Sangihe Islands") and [Talaud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talaud "Talaud")). Also mentioned are [Makassar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makassar "Makassar"), [Buton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buton "Buton"), [Banggai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banggai_Regency "Banggai Regency"), Kunir, [Galiao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pantar "Pantar") with [Selayar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selayar_Islands "Selayar Islands"), [Sumba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sumba "Sumba"), [Solot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sulu_archipelago "Sulu archipelago"), [Muar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muar_(town) "Muar (town)"). Also Wanda(n) ( [Banda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banda_Islands "Banda Islands") island), [Ambon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ambon_Island "Ambon Island") or [Maluku islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maluku_Islands "Maluku Islands"), [Kai Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kai_Islands "Kai Islands"), Wanin ( [Onin Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Onin_Peninsula?action=edit&redlink=1 "Onin Peninsula (page does not exist)"), today [Fakfak Regency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fakfak_Regency "Fakfak Regency"), West Papua), [Sran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Kaimana "Kingdom of Kaimana"), [Timor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timor "Timor") and other islands.

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b6/Asia_in_1335.svg/500px-Asia_in_1335.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Asia_in_1335.svg) Asia in the early 14th century

The true nature of Majapahit [suzerainty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suzerainty "Suzerainty") is still a subject of study and even has sparked controversy. Nagarakretagama describes Majapahit as the centre of a huge [mandala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandala_(political_model) "Mandala (political model)") consists of 98 tributaries stretching from Sumatra to New Guinea. Some scholars have discounted this claim as merely a sphere of limited influence, or even just a statement of geographical knowledge.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Cribb-2)[\[7\]](#refnote-Cribb):87

Nevertheless, the Javanese overseas prestige and influence during the lifetime of Hayam Wuruk was undoubtedly considerable.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Britannica-Majapahit-3)[\[10\]](#refnote-Britannica-Majapahit) Majapahit fleets must have periodically visited many places in the archipelago to acquire formal submission, or the splendour of Majapahit court might have attracted regional rulers to send a tribute, without any intention to submit to Majapahit's order.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Cribb-3)[\[7\]](#refnote-Cribb):87

All of those three categories–the _Negara Agung, Mancanegara_ and _Nusantara_, were within the sphere of influence of the Majapahit empire.

The model of political formations and power diffusion from its core in the Majapahit capital city that radiates through its overseas possessions was later identified by historians as " [mandala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandala_(Southeast_Asian_history) "Mandala (Southeast Asian history)")" model. The term mandala derived from Sanskrit "circle" to explain the typical ancient Southeast Asian polity that was defined by its centre rather than its boundaries, and it could be composed of numerous other tributary polities without undergoing administrative integration.[&#8202;](#ref-back-123-0)[\[106\]](#refnote-123) The territories belongs within Majapahit Mandala sphere of influence were those categorised as Mancanegara and Nusantara. These areas usually have their indigenous rulers, enjoy substantial autonomy and have their political institution intact without further integration into Majapahit administration. The same mandala model also applied to previous empires; [Srivijaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Srivijaya "Srivijaya") and [Angkor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khmer_Empire "Khmer Empire"), and also Majapahit's neighbouring mandalas; Ayutthaya and Champa.

Foreign or overseas territories are mentioned in Nagarakretagama canto 15 stanza 1. These territories include Syangka (Siam), Ayodyapura ( [Ayutthaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayutthaya_Kingdom "Ayutthaya Kingdom")), Dharmmanagari ( [Ligor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ligor "Ligor")), Marutma ( [Martaban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martaban "Martaban") or [Mergui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mergui "Mergui")), Rajapura ( [Rajpuri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ratchaburi_province "Ratchaburi province") in the south of Siam), Singhanagari ( [Singhapuri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singburi "Singburi") on the branch of the river Menam), Campa, and Kamboja ( [Cambodia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambodia "Cambodia")).[&#8202;](#ref-back-Pigeaud_1962-0)[\[107\]](#refnote-Pigeaud_1962):35–36 The relationship between Majapahit and these territories is called _kachaya_, which means "to be exposed to light". It is interpreted as protected or sheltered. The term "protected area" in the modern state system is referred to as a [protectorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protectorate "Protectorate").[&#8202;](#ref-back-:152-0)[\[108\]](#refnote-:152):234–235

In addition, in canto 83 stanza 4 and 93 stanza 1 are mentioned the places that became the origin of merchants and scholars. The regions are Jambudwipa (India), Cina, [Karnataka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karnataka "Karnataka") (South India), and Goda ( [Gauḍa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gau%E1%B8%8Da_(city) "Gauḍa (city)")).[&#8202;](#ref-back-Pigeaud_1962-1)[\[107\]](#refnote-Pigeaud_1962):35–36 What is different is Yawana ( [Arabs, see explanation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territories_of_Majapahit#Overseas_region "Territories of Majapahit")), as mentioned _anyat i yawana mitreka satata_ (different is Yawana who is a permanent ally).[&#8202;](#ref-back-:152-1)[\[108\]](#refnote-:152):234[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTENugroho2011141–142-0)[\[109\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTENugroho2011141–142)

The question whether Majapahit is considered an [empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire "Empire") or not is actually depends on the definition of the word and concept of "empire" itself. Majapahit did not performed direct administration of its overseas possessions, they did not maintain permanent military occupation, and did not imposes its political and cultural norms over a wide area; thus it is not sufficiently considered an empire in a traditional sense. However, if being an empire means the projection of military power at will, formal acknowledgement of overlordship by vassals, and the regular tribute delivery to the capital, then Java's relationship to the rest of archipelagic realm can well be considered an imperial one; thus Majapahit can be considered an empire.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Sastrawan_Empire-3)[\[1\]](#refnote-Sastrawan_Empire)

In later period, Majapahit's hold on its overseas possessions began to wane. According to [Waringin Pitu inscription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waringin_Pitu_inscription?action=edit&redlink=1 "Waringin Pitu inscription (page does not exist)") (dated 1447) it was mentioned that Majapahit's core realm was consisted of 14 provinces, that administrated by the ruler titled _Bhre_.[&#8202;](#ref-back-127-0)[\[110\]](#refnote-127) The provinces written in the inscription are:

- [Daha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kediri_(historical_kingdom) "Kediri (historical kingdom)") (former capital of [Kediri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kediri_(historical_kingdom) "Kediri (historical kingdom)"))
- Jagaraga
- Kabalan
- [Kahuripan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kahuripan "Kahuripan") (or [Janggala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Janggala "Janggala"), modern [Surabaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surabaya "Surabaya"))
- Keling
- Kelinggapura
- Kembang Jenar
- [Matahun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matahun?action=edit&redlink=1 "Matahun (page does not exist)") (today [Bojonegoro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bojonegoro "Bojonegoro"))
- [Pajang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pajang "Pajang") (today [Surakarta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surakarta "Surakarta"))
- [Singhapura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cirebon "Cirebon")
- [Tanjungpura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanjungpura_Kingdom "Tanjungpura Kingdom")
- [Tumapel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tumapel "Tumapel") (former capital of [Singhasari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singhasari "Singhasari"))
- [Wengker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wengker?action=edit&redlink=1 "Wengker (page does not exist)") (today [Ponorogo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ponorogo "Ponorogo"))
- [Wirabhumi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wirabhumi?action=edit&redlink=1 "Wirabhumi (page does not exist)") (today [Blambangan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blambangan "Blambangan"))

The inscription, however, did not mention Majapahit's vassals in other areas around the period, such as:

- [Indragiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indragiri_(disambiguation) "Indragiri (disambiguation)") in Sumatra and [Siantan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siantan?action=edit&redlink=1 "Siantan (page does not exist)") (now in [Pontianak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontianak "Pontianak") in the west coast of Borneo), which according to the [Malay Annals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_Annals "Malay Annals"), were given as a dowry to [Malacca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Malacca "Sultanate of Malacca") for the marriage of a Majapahit princess and [Mansur Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mansur_Shah "Mansur Shah") sometime during his reign.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Leyden-1)[\[72\]](#refnote-Leyden) Sultan [Mansur Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mansur_Shah "Mansur Shah") reigned from 1459 to 1477,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Coedes-9)[\[32\]](#refnote-Coedes) which implies that Indragiri and Siantan were both still under Majapahit's control in 1447.
- [Jambi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jambi "Jambi") and [Palembang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palembang "Palembang"), which only ceased to be under Majapahit suzerainty when seized by [Demak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demak_Sultanate "Demak Sultanate").[&#8202;](#ref-back-:3-3)[\[77\]](#refnote-:3):154–155
- And [Bali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bali "Bali"), which were known to be the last stronghold of the Hindu-Javanese civilization during Majapahit's downfall.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Coedes-10)[\[32\]](#refnote-Coedes)

The book _[Suma Oriental](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suma_Oriental "Suma Oriental")_ by Tomé Pires written in 1515 records that Java (Majapahit) ruled as far as the Moluccas on the east side and most of the west side of the archipelago; and almost the whole island of Sumatra was under its control and also controlled all the islands known to the Javanese. Majapahit ruled over these for a long time until about a hundred years earlier, when its power began to wane until it became similar to that during the year of Pires' visit to Java (March–June 1513).[&#8202;](#ref-back-:3-4)[\[77\]](#refnote-:3):174

## Military

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Majapahit&action=edit&section=32 "Edit section: Military")\]

| Conflicts involving Majapahit |
| --- |
| 1200’s<br>- [Pamalayu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pamalayu "Pamalayu")<br>- [Mongol invasion of Java](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_invasion_of_Java "Mongol invasion of Java")<br>- [Ranggalawe rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ranggalawe_rebellion?action=edit&redlink=1 "Ranggalawe rebellion (page does not exist)")<br>1300’<br>- [Ra Kuti uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ra_Kuti_uprising "Ra Kuti uprising")<br>- [Sadeng rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sadeng_rebellion?action=edit&redlink=1 "Sadeng rebellion (page does not exist)")<br>- [Nambi rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nambi_rebellion?action=edit&redlink=1 "Nambi rebellion (page does not exist)")<br>- [Majapahit invasions of Nan Sarunai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majapahit_invasions_of_Nan_Sarunai "Majapahit invasions of Nan Sarunai")<br>- [Battle of Bubat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Bubat "Battle of Bubat")<br>- [Sack of Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sack_of_Singapore "Sack of Singapore")<br>1400’s-1500’s<br>- [Regreg war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regreg_war "Regreg war")<br>- [Demak–Majapahit conflicts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demak%E2%80%93Majapahit_conflicts "Demak–Majapahit conflicts")<br>- [Majapahit–Sundanese conflicts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majapahit%E2%80%93Sundanese_conflicts "Majapahit–Sundanese conflicts")<br>- [Malacca-Majapahit conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malacca-Majapahit_conflict "Malacca-Majapahit conflict") |

The army of Majapahit was divided into 2 main types, namely _prajurit_ (professional soldier) and [levy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conscription#Medieval_levies "Conscription") taken up from peasants. The main weapon used was the spear.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:15-0)[\[111\]](#refnote-:15):84–85,130 Initially, cavalry only existed in limited numbers. They were mainly used for scouting and patrol, and were most likely armed with lances.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:15-1)[\[111\]](#refnote-:15):90,94 After the Mongol invasion, the use of horses became more widespread in Java, especially for war.[&#8202;](#ref-back-129-0)[\[112\]](#refnote-129):12–13[Chariots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chariot "Chariot") are used for transporting soldiers to the battlefield and were "parked" before the battle. Some chariots were indeed used in battle, for example, the prime minister Nambi rode a chariot and served as an archer in the Rangga Lawe rebellion (1295 CE). Gajah Mada also rode in a chariot when attacking Sundanese troops in the [Bubat battle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Bubat "Battle of Bubat") (1357). Chariots were carved at the Penataran Temple, seemingly modeled from the real world.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:15-2)[\[111\]](#refnote-:15):96,98[&#8202;](#ref-back-:0222-0)[\[113\]](#refnote-:0222):64,76[&#8202;](#ref-back-:17-0)[\[114\]](#refnote-:17):141,142[War elephants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_elephant "War elephant") were used mainly for transport, or as a mount for nobility and soldiers of higher rank.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:15-3)[\[111\]](#refnote-:15):101

Majapahit had 30,000 full-time professional troops, whose soldiers and commanders were paid in gold. This shows the existence of a [standing army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standing_army "Standing army"), an achievement that only a handful of Southeast Asian empires could hope to achieve.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:62-0)[\[115\]](#refnote-:62):185[&#8202;](#ref-back-:022-0)[\[116\]](#refnote-:022):467 In addition to these professional soldiers, Majapahit was strengthened by troops from subordinate countries and regional leaders.[&#8202;](#ref-back-end-1)[\[51\]](#refnote-end):277 From the records of _Suma Oriental_ and Malay Annals, the total number of Majapahit troops could reach 200,000 people.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:33-0)[\[117\]](#refnote-:33):175–176[&#8202;](#ref-back-135-0)[\[118\]](#refnote-135) The Majapahit troops were multiethnic, similar to the [Yogyakarta Sultanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yogyakarta_Sultanate "Yogyakarta Sultanate") military which had [Bugis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bugis "Bugis") and Dhaeng ( [Makassar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makassar_people "Makassar people")) troops. As noted in _Hikayat Raja-Raja Pasai_:

> So the two parties returned to their respective places. That was how the war went on every day, for about three months, the war was unrelenting, because the Javanese kingdom also received help from foreign lands.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTENugroho2011182-0)[\[119\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTENugroho2011182)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/39/Bedil_kuno_atau_meriam_kuno_Jawa_cetbang.png/250px-Bedil_kuno_atau_meriam_kuno_Jawa_cetbang.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Bedil_kuno_atau_meriam_kuno_Jawa_cetbang.png)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dc/Madrid_canons_indiens.png/250px-Madrid_canons_indiens.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Madrid_canons_indiens.png)

Gunpowder weapons used by Majapahit:

- Bronze hand cannon-type [cetbang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cetbang "Cetbang"), found in the Brantas river, Jombang
- A double-barrelled cetbang on a carriage, with swivel yoke, c. 1522. The mouth of the cannon is in the shape of [Javanese Nāga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N%C4%81ga#Indonesia "Nāga").

[Gunpowder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gunpowder "Gunpowder") technology entered Java in the [Mongol invasion of Java](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_invasion_of_Java "Mongol invasion of Java") (1293 A.D.).[&#8202;](#ref-back-137-0)[\[120\]](#refnote-137)[&#8202;](#ref-back-138-0)[\[121\]](#refnote-138):220 Majapahit under _Mahapatih_ (prime minister) [Gajah Mada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gajah_Mada "Gajah Mada") utilized gunpowder technology obtained from the [Yuan dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuan_dynasty "Yuan dynasty") for use in the naval fleet.[&#8202;](#ref-back-139-0)[\[122\]](#refnote-139) During the following years, the Majapahit army have begun producing cannons known as [cetbang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cetbang "Cetbang"). Early cetbang (also called eastern-style cetbang) resembled Chinese cannons and hand cannons. Eastern-style cetbangs were mostly made of bronze and were front-loaded cannons. It fires arrow-like projectiles, but round bullets and co-viative projectiles[&#8202;](#ref-back-141-0)[\[note 18\]](#refnote-141) can also be used. These arrows can be solid-tipped without explosives, or with explosives and incendiary materials placed behind the tip. Near the rear, there is a combustion chamber or room, which refers to the bulging part near the rear of the gun, where the gunpowder is placed. The cetbang is mounted on a fixed mount, or as a hand cannon mounted on the end of a pole. There is a tube-like section on the back of the cannon. In the hand cannon-type cetbang, this tube is used as a socket for a pole.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:102-0)[\[124\]](#refnote-:102):94

Because of the close maritime relations of the Nusantara archipelago with the territory of west India, after 1460 new types of gunpowder weapons entered the archipelago through Arab intermediaries. This weapon seems to be cannon and gun of Ottoman tradition, for example the [prangi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prangi "Prangi"), which is a [breech-loading swivel gun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breech-loading_swivel_gun "Breech-loading swivel gun").[&#8202;](#ref-back-:102-1)[\[124\]](#refnote-:102):94–95 It resulted in a new type of cetbang, called "western-style cetbang". It can be mounted as a fixed or swivel gun, small-sized ones can be easily installed on small vessels. In naval combat, this gun is used as an anti-personnel weapon, not anti-ship. In this age, even to the 17th century, Nusantaran soldiers fought on a platform called _balai_ and performed boarding actions. Loaded with scatter shots ( [grapeshot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grapeshot "Grapeshot"), [case shot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Case_shot "Case shot"), or [nails](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nail_(fastener) "Nail (fastener)") and stones) and fired at close range, the cetbang would have been effective at this type of fighting.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:72-0)[\[125\]](#refnote-:72):241[&#8202;](#ref-back-:02-0)[\[126\]](#refnote-:02):162

Majapahit had elite troops called _Bhayangkara_. The main task of these troops is for protecting the king and nobilities, but they may also be deployed to the battlefield if required. The [Hikayat Banjar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hikayat_Banjar "Hikayat Banjar") noted the _Bhayangkara_ equipments in the Majapahit palace:

> _Maka kaluar dangan parhiasannya orang barbaju-rantai ampat puluh sarta padangnya barkupiah taranggos sakhlat merah, orang mambawa astenggar ampat puluh, orang mambawa parisai sarta padangnya ampat puluh, orang mambawa dadap sarta sodoknya sapuluh, orang mambawa panah sarta anaknya sapuluh, yang mambawa tumbak parampukan barsulam amas ampat puluh, yang mambawa tameng Bali bartulis air mas ampat puluh._
>
> So came out with their ornaments men with [chain mail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chain_mail "Chain mail") numbered forty alongside their swords and red _[kopiah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kopiah "Kopiah")_ \[skull cap\], men carrying _astengger_ \[ [arquebus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arquebus "Arquebus")\] numbered forty, men carrying shield and swords numbered forty, men carrying _dadap_ \[&#8202;](#ref-back-148-0)[a type of shield\][\[note 19\]](#refnote-148) and _sodok_ \[&#8202;](#ref-back-150-0)[broad-bladed spear-like weapon\][\[note 20\]](#refnote-150) numbered ten, men carrying bows and arrows numbered ten, (men) who carried _parampukan_ spears[&#8202;](#ref-back-152-0)[\[note 21\]](#refnote-152) embroidered with gold numbered forty, (men) who carried Balinese shields with golden water engraving numbered forty.
>
> – Hikayat Banjar, 6.3[&#8202;](#ref-back-153-0)[\[132\]](#refnote-153):Line 1209–1214[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTENugroho2011204-0)[\[133\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTENugroho2011204)

- [![Various keris and pole weapons of Java](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1d/Archipel_Asiatique_Malaisie_-_Armes_Offensives_et_%C3%89tendard.jpg/250px-Archipel_Asiatique_Malaisie_-_Armes_Offensives_et_%C3%89tendard.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Archipel_Asiatique_Malaisie_-_Armes_Offensives_et_%C3%89tendard.jpg "Various keris and pole weapons of Java")

Various keris and pole weapons of Java

- [![Bodhisattva Manjusri wielding a sword, from Candi Jago, 1343](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/65/Plaster_cast_of_the_bodhisattwa_Manjusri_from_candi_Jago_dated_1265_Saka_or_1343_CE.jpg/250px-Plaster_cast_of_the_bodhisattwa_Manjusri_from_candi_Jago_dated_1265_Saka_or_1343_CE.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Plaster_cast_of_the_bodhisattwa_Manjusri_from_candi_Jago_dated_1265_Saka_or_1343_CE.jpg "Bodhisattva Manjusri wielding a sword, from Candi Jago, 1343")

Bodhisattva Manjusri wielding a sword, from [Candi Jago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Candi_Jago "Candi Jago"), 1343

Military forces in various parts of Southeast Asia were lightly armored. As was common in Southeast Asia, most of the Javanese forces were composed of temporarily conscripted commoners (levy) led by the warrior and noble castes. The "peasant army" was usually bare-chested wearing a _[sarung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarong "Sarong")_, armed with spear, short sword, or bow and arrows.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:13-0)[\[134\]](#refnote-:13):111–113 The richer (higher rank) soldiers wore armor called _[kawaca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kawaca "Kawaca")_.[&#8202;](#ref-back-157-0)[\[note 22\]](#refnote-157)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:15-4)[\[111\]](#refnote-:15):78 Irawan Djoko Nugroho argues that it may be shaped like a long tube and was made of cast copper.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTENugroho2011202,_386-0)[\[136\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTENugroho2011202,_386) In contrast, the regular infantry (professional soldiers, not the levy) wore a scale armor called _[siping-siping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siping-siping "Siping-siping")_.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:15-5)[\[111\]](#refnote-:15):75,78–79 There is also a kind of steel helmet called _rukuh_.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTENugroho2011321-0)[\[137\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTENugroho2011321)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:15-6)[\[111\]](#refnote-:15):20,78,80 Other kinds of armor used in Majapahit-era Java was _[waju rante](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baju_Rantai "Baju Rantai")_ (chain mail armor) and _[karambalangan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karambalangan "Karambalangan")_ (a layer of metal worn in front of the chest).[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTENugroho2011202,_320-0)[\[138\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTENugroho2011202,_320)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:8-0)[\[139\]](#refnote-:8)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:5-0)[\[140\]](#refnote-:5) In [Kidung Sunda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kidung_Sunda "Kidung Sunda") canto 2 stanza 85 it is explained that the _mantri_ s (ministers or officers) of Gajah Mada wore armor in the form of chain mail or breastplate with gold decoration and dressed in yellow attire,[&#8202;](#ref-back-:7-0)[\[141\]](#refnote-:7):103 while Kidung Sundayana canto 1 stanza 95 mentioned that Gajah Mada wore golden embossed _karambalangan_, armed with gold-layered spear, and with a shield full of diamond decoration.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:5-1)[\[140\]](#refnote-:5)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:8-1)[\[139\]](#refnote-:8)

Majapahit also pioneered the use of firearms in the archipelago. Even though the knowledge of making gunpowder-based weapons has been known after the failed Mongol invasion of Java, and the predecessor of firearms, the [pole gun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hand_cannon "Hand cannon") ( [bedil tombak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bedil_tombak "Bedil tombak")) was recorded by Ma Huan in his [_Yingya Shenlan_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yingya_Shenglan "Yingya Shenglan") as being used by the [Javanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Javanese_people "Javanese people") for marriage ceremony in 1413,[&#8202;](#ref-back-164-0)[\[142\]](#refnote-164)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:72-1)[\[125\]](#refnote-:72):245 the knowledge of making "true" firearms came much later, after the middle of the 15th century. It was brought by the [Islamic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam "Islam") nations of West Asia, most probably the [Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabs "Arabs"). The precise year of introduction is unknown, but it may be safely concluded to be no earlier than 1460.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:22-0)[\[143\]](#refnote-:22):23

_[Xingcha Shenglan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xingcha_Shenglan "Xingcha Shenglan")_ (星槎勝覽) written by [Fei Xin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fei_Xin "Fei Xin") c. 1436 stated that Java (Majapahit) is equipped with armored soldiers and equipment, and it is the center of the Eastern people.[&#8202;](#ref-back-166-0)[\[144\]](#refnote-166)[&#8202;](#ref-back-167-0)[\[145\]](#refnote-167) _Haiguo Guangji_ (海国广记) and _Shuyu zhouzi lu_ (殊域周咨錄) recorded that Java is vast and densely populated, and their armored soldiers and hand cannons (火銃—huǒ chòng) dominated the Eastern Seas.[&#8202;](#ref-back-168-0)[\[146\]](#refnote-168):755[&#8202;](#ref-back-169-0)[\[147\]](#refnote-169)[&#8202;](#ref-back-170-0)[\[148\]](#refnote-170)

Tomé Pires' 1513 account tells the army of Gusti Pati (Patih Udara), viceroy of _Batara Vojyaya_ (probably Brawijaya or Ranawijaya), numbered 200,000 men, 2,000 of which are horsemen and 4,000 musketeers.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:3-5)[\[77\]](#refnote-:3):175–176 Duarte Barbosa ca. 1514 recorded that the inhabitants of Java are great masters in casting artillery and very good artillerymen. They make many one-pounder cannons (cetbang or [rentaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lantaka "Lantaka")), long muskets, _spingarde_ (arquebus), _schioppi_ (hand cannon), [Greek fire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_fire "Greek fire"), guns (cannons), and other fire-works.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:4-0)[\[149\]](#refnote-:4):198[&#8202;](#ref-back-172-0)[\[150\]](#refnote-172):224 Every place is considered excellent in casting artillery, and in the knowledge of using it.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:14-0)[\[151\]](#refnote-:14):254[&#8202;](#ref-back-:4-1)[\[149\]](#refnote-:4):198

Reliefs at the main temple of the Penataran temple complex, 1269 Śaka or 1347 CE.

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/Close-up_of_KITLV_87835_-_Isidore_van_Kinsbergen_-_Relief_on_Tjandi_Panataran_near_Blitar_-_Before_1900.jpg/960px-Close-up_of_KITLV_87835_-_Isidore_van_Kinsbergen_-_Relief_on_Tjandi_Panataran_near_Blitar_-_Before_1900.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Close-up_of_KITLV_87835_-_Isidore_van_Kinsbergen_-_Relief_on_Tjandi_Panataran_near_Blitar_-_Before_1900.jpg)

Worn-out reliefs in Candi Penataran, showing an armored horse rider, armored warriors, and a chariot.

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f2/Close-up_of_KITLV_87823_-_Isidore_van_Kinsbergen_-_Reliefs_on_Tjandi_Panataran_near_Blitar_-_Before_1900.jpg/960px-Close-up_of_KITLV_87823_-_Isidore_van_Kinsbergen_-_Reliefs_on_Tjandi_Panataran_near_Blitar_-_Before_1900.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Close-up_of_KITLV_87823_-_Isidore_van_Kinsbergen_-_Reliefs_on_Tjandi_Panataran_near_Blitar_-_Before_1900.jpg)

Battle scene from the relief of the Krishnayana story.

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9f/Close-up_of_KITLV_28323_-_Isidore_van_Kinsbergen_-_Relief_on_the_first_gallery_on_the_south_side_of_Panataran_near_Blitar_-_1867-02-1867-06.jpg/960px-Close-up_of_KITLV_28323_-_Isidore_van_Kinsbergen_-_Relief_on_the_first_gallery_on_the_south_side_of_Panataran_near_Blitar_-_1867-02-1867-06.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Close-up_of_KITLV_28323_-_Isidore_van_Kinsbergen_-_Relief_on_the_first_gallery_on_the_south_side_of_Panataran_near_Blitar_-_1867-02-1867-06.jpg)

Troops consisting of war chariots, spearmen, and a war elephant.

The first true cavalry (organized units of cooperative horsemen) in Java may have appeared during the 12th century CE.[&#8202;](#ref-back-174-0)[\[152\]](#refnote-174) The old Javanese manuscript _kakawin Bhomāntaka_ mentioned early Javanese horse lore and horsemanship.[&#8202;](#ref-back-175-0)[\[153\]](#refnote-175) The manuscript may reflect the conflict (allegorically) between the newly formed Javanese cavalry and the well-established elite infantry of Javanese armies.[&#8202;](#ref-back-176-0)[\[154\]](#refnote-176) In the 14th century CE, Java became an important horse breeder and the island was even listed among horse suppliers to China.[&#8202;](#ref-back-177-0)[\[155\]](#refnote-177) During the Majapahit period, the quantity and quality of [Javanese horse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Java_Pony "Java Pony") breeds steadily grew. In 1513 CE Tomé Pires praised the highly decorated horses of Javanese nobility, complemented by gold-studded stirrups and lavishly decorated saddles that were "not found anywhere else in the world".[&#8202;](#ref-back-:3-6)[\[77\]](#refnote-:3):174–175[&#8202;](#ref-back-178-0)[\[156\]](#refnote-178) The [Sumbawa pony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sumbawa_pony "Sumbawa pony") seems to have been derived from the Javanese domesticated horse introduced by Majapahit in the 14th century CE.[&#8202;](#ref-back-179-0)[\[157\]](#refnote-179)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e3/231_Krishnayana_Reliefs_%2840388020252%29.jpg/250px-231_Krishnayana_Reliefs_%2840388020252%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:231_Krishnayana_Reliefs_(40388020252).jpg)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/20/Close-up_of_KITLV_28301_-_Isidore_van_Kinsbergen_-_Relief_with_part_of_the_Ramayana_epic_on_the_south_side_of_Panataran%2C_Kediri_-_1867-02-1867-06_warrior_and_horse_archer.jpg/120px-thumbnail.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Close-up_of_KITLV_28301_-_Isidore_van_Kinsbergen_-_Relief_with_part_of_the_Ramayana_epic_on_the_south_side_of_Panataran,_Kediri_-_1867-02-1867-06_warrior_and_horse_archer.jpg)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/15/Close-up_of_KITLV_28291_-_Isidore_van_Kinsbergen_-_Relief_on_the_first_gallery_on_the_north_side_of_Panataran_near_Blitar_-_1867-02-1867-06_horse_riders.jpg/250px-Close-up_of_KITLV_28291_-_Isidore_van_Kinsbergen_-_Relief_on_the_first_gallery_on_the_north_side_of_Panataran_near_Blitar_-_1867-02-1867-06_horse_riders.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Close-up_of_KITLV_28291_-_Isidore_van_Kinsbergen_-_Relief_on_the_first_gallery_on_the_north_side_of_Panataran_near_Blitar_-_1867-02-1867-06_horse_riders.jpg)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/214_Krishnayana_Reliefs_%2826560692388%29.jpg/250px-214_Krishnayana_Reliefs_%2826560692388%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:214_Krishnayana_Reliefs_(26560692388).jpg)

Soldiers and equipments depicted in the main temple of Penataran

Majapahit had a naval troops that was distinguished from ground troop units, which was called _wwang jaladhi_. Naval troops get preferential treatment in terms of facilities. Majapahit navy personnel were large, as noted Nagarakretagama canto 16 stanza 5:[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTENugroho2011178–179-0)[\[158\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTENugroho2011178–179)[&#8202;](#ref-back-181-0)[\[159\]](#refnote-181):17,148,281[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEPigeaud1960c19-0)[\[160\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEPigeaud1960c19)

> _irika tang anyabhumi sakhahemban ing Yawapuri,_ (Then 'Anyabhumi' \[other lands\] everywhere were all united in the Javanese kingdom,)
>
> _amateh i sajna sang nrpati khapwa satya ring ulah,_ (obey every command of the king. All are faithful in attitude,)
>
> _pituwi sing ajñalanghyana dinon wiśirnna sahana,_ (even though there were treaty violators, they were attacked by expeditionary forces and were all destroyed,)
>
> _tekap ikang watek jaladhi mantry aneka suyaśa._ (by the activity of the group of 'mantri jaladhi' \[naval officers\] who were numerous, glorious.)

The main warship of the Majapahit navy was the [jong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Djong_(ship) "Djong (ship)"). The jongs were large transport ships that could carry 100–2000 tons of cargo and 50–1000 people. They are about 26.36–80.51 m in [LOD (deck length)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Length_on_deck "Length on deck") and 28.99–88.56 m in [LOA (overall length)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Length_overall "Length overall"). A jong from 1420 nearly crossed the Atlantic Ocean.[&#8202;](#ref-back-183-0)[\[161\]](#refnote-183)\[ _[bettersourceneeded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability#Questionable_sources "Wikipedia:Verifiability")_\] A type of large nine-decked jong that was recorded in _Kidung_ _Panji Wijayakrama-Rangga Lawe_ (c. 1334) was called _jong sasangawangunan_, it carried 1000 fighting men with red sails.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:17-1)[\[114\]](#refnote-:17):91 The exact number of jongs fielded by Majapahit is unknown, but the largest number of jongs deployed in an expedition was about 400 jongs when Majapahit attacked Pasai.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:2-1)[\[44\]](#refnote-:2) The average jong used by Majapahit would be about 76.18–79.81 m [LOA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Length_overall "Length overall"), carrying 600–700 men, with 1200–1400 tons [deadweight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deadweight_tonnage "Deadweight tonnage").[&#8202;](#ref-back-184-0)[\[162\]](#refnote-184):60–62 Before the [Battle of Bubat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Bubat "Battle of Bubat") in 1357, the [Sunda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunda_Kingdom "Sunda Kingdom") king and the royal family arrived in Majapahit after sailing across the [Java Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Java_Sea "Java Sea") in a fleet of 200 large ships and 2000 smaller vessels.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:7-1)[\[141\]](#refnote-:7):16–17,76–77 The royal family boarded a nine-decked hybrid Sino-Southeast Asian junk (Old Javanese: _Jong sasanga wangunan_ _ring Tatarnagari tiniru_). This hybrid junk incorporated Chinese techniques, such as using iron nails alongside wooden dowels, the construction of watertight bulkheads, and the addition of a central rudder.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:32-0)[\[163\]](#refnote-:32):270[&#8202;](#ref-back-:6-0)[\[164\]](#refnote-:6):272–276 Besides that, other types of vessels used by Majapahit navy are [malangbang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malangbang "Malangbang"), [kelulus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kelulus "Kelulus"), [pelang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pelang "Pelang"), [jongkong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jongkong "Jongkong"), [cerucuh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cerucuh "Cerucuh"), and [tongkang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tongkang "Tongkang").[&#8202;](#ref-back-:2-2)[\[44\]](#refnote-:2)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:10-1)[\[64\]](#refnote-:10)[&#8202;](#ref-back-187-0)[\[165\]](#refnote-187) In the 16th century [lancaran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lancaran_(ship) "Lancaran (ship)") and [penjajap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penjajap "Penjajap") are also used.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:3-7)[\[77\]](#refnote-:3):195[&#8202;](#ref-back-188-0)[\[166\]](#refnote-188):282 Modern depictions of the Majapahit navy often depict [outrigger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outrigger "Outrigger") ships, in reality, these ships were from 8th-century [Borobudur ship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borobudur_ship "Borobudur ship") bas relief. Research by Nugroho concluded that the main vessels used by Majapahit did not use outriggers, and using [Borobudur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borobudur "Borobudur") engraving as the basis for reconstructing the Majapahit ship is wrong and misleading.[&#8202;](#ref-back-189-0)[\[167\]](#refnote-189)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTENugroho2011266–267-0)[\[168\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTENugroho2011266–267)

## Explorations and navigation

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Majapahit&action=edit&section=33 "Edit section: Explorations and navigation")\]

During the Majapahit era, Nusantaran exploration reached its greatest accomplishment. [Ludovico di Varthema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ludovico_di_Varthema "Ludovico di Varthema") (1470–1517), in his book _Itinerario de Ludouico de Varthema Bolognese_ stated that the Southern Javanese people sailed to "far Southern lands" up to the point they arrived at an island where a day only lasted four hours long and was "colder than in any part of the world". Modern studies have determined that such place is located at least 900 nautical miles (1666km) south of the southernmost point of [Tasmania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tasmania "Tasmania").[&#8202;](#ref-back-:9-0)[\[169\]](#refnote-:9):248–251

The Javanese people, like other [Austronesian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austronesian_peoples "Austronesian peoples") ethnicities, use a solid navigation system: Orientation at sea is carried out using a variety of different natural signs, and by using a very distinctive [astronomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astronomy "Astronomy") technique called " [star path navigation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Star_navigation "Star navigation")". Basically, the navigators determine the bow of the ship to the islands that are recognized by using the position of rising and setting of certain stars above the horizon.[&#8202;](#ref-back-192-0)[\[170\]](#refnote-192):10 In the Majapahit era, [compasses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compass "Compass") and [magnets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnet "Magnet") were used, and [cartography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cartography "Cartography") (mapping science) was developed. In 1293 CE Raden Wijaya presented a map and census record to the [Yuan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuan_dynasty "Yuan dynasty") Mongol invader, suggesting that mapmaking has been a formal part of governmental affairs in Java.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:12-1)[\[36\]](#refnote-:12):53 The use of maps full of longitudinal and transverse lines, rhumb lines, and direct route lines traveled by ships were recorded by Europeans, to the point that the Portuguese considered the Javanese maps were the best map in the early 1500s.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:9-1)[\[169\]](#refnote-:9):249[&#8202;](#ref-back-:3-8)[\[77\]](#refnote-:3):lxxix[&#8202;](#ref-back-:0-0)[\[171\]](#refnote-:0)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTENugroho2011163–164,_166–168-0)[\[172\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTENugroho2011163–164,_166–168)[&#8202;](#ref-back-195-0)[\[173\]](#refnote-195)[&#8202;](#ref-back-196-0)[\[174\]](#refnote-196)

When [Afonso de Albuquerque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afonso_de_Albuquerque "Afonso de Albuquerque") conquered [Malacca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malacca_Sultanate "Malacca Sultanate") (1511), the Portuguese recovered a chart from a Javanese [maritime pilot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maritime_pilot "Maritime pilot"), which already included part of the [Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Americas "Americas"). Regarding the chart Albuquerque said:[&#8202;](#ref-back-:52-0)[\[175\]](#refnote-:52)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:0-1)[\[171\]](#refnote-:0):98–99

> "...a large map of a Javanese pilot, containing the [Cape of Good Hope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_of_Good_Hope "Cape of Good Hope"), [Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Portugal "Kingdom of Portugal") and the land of [Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_Brazil "Colonial Brazil"), the [Red Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Sea "Red Sea") and the [Sea of Persia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_Gulf "Persian Gulf"), the [Clove Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maluku_Islands "Maluku Islands"), the navigation of the Chinese and the Gores, with their rhumbs and direct routes followed by the ships, and the hinterland, and how the kingdoms border on each other. It seems to me. Sir, that this was the best thing I have ever seen, and Your Highness will be very pleased to see it; it had the names in Javanese writing, but I had with me a Javanese who could read and write. I send this piece to Your Highness, which Francisco Rodrigues traced from the other, in which Your Highness can truly see where the [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_people "Chinese people") and [Gores](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ryukyuan_people "Ryukyuan people") come from, and the course your ships must take to the Clove Islands, and where the gold mines lie, and the islands of [Java](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Java "Java") and [Banda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banda_Islands "Banda Islands"), of nutmeg and mace, and the land of the King of [Siam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayutthaya_Kingdom "Ayutthaya Kingdom"), and also the end of the land of the navigation of the Chinese, the direction it takes, and how they do not navigate farther."
>
> — Letter of Albuquerque to [King Manuel I of Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_I_of_Portugal "Manuel I of Portugal"), 1 April 1512.

Duarte Barbosa mention places and route which the Majapahit ships visited, which include [Maluku Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maluku_Islands "Maluku Islands"), [Timor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timor "Timor"), [Banda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banda_Islands "Banda Islands"), [Sumatra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sumatra "Sumatra"), [Malacca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_Peninsula "Malay Peninsula"), [China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_China "Greater China"), [Tenasserim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tenasserim_coast "Tenasserim coast"), Pegu ( [Bago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bago_Region "Bago Region")), [Bengal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengal "Bengal"), [Pulicat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulicat "Pulicat"), [Coromandel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coromandel_Coast "Coromandel Coast"), [Malabar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malabar_Coast "Malabar Coast"), Cambay ( [Khambat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khambhat "Khambhat")), and [Aden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aden "Aden"). From the notes of other authors, it is known that there were also those who went to the [Maldives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atolls_of_the_Maldives "Atolls of the Maldives"), Calicut ( [Kozhikode](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kozhikode "Kozhikode")), [Oman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imamate_of_Oman "Imamate of Oman"), [Aden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aden "Aden"), and the [Red Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Sea "Red Sea"). The passenger brought their wives and children, even some of them never leave the ship to go on shore, nor have any other dwelling, for they are born and die in the ship.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:4-2)[\[149\]](#refnote-:4):191–193[&#8202;](#ref-back-:16-0)[\[176\]](#refnote-:16):199[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTENugroho2011289–290-0)[\[177\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTENugroho2011289–290)[&#8202;](#ref-back-end-2)[\[51\]](#refnote-end):278

## Relations with regional powers

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Majapahit&action=edit&section=34 "Edit section: Relations with regional powers")\]

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bf/JavaneseGoldOrnaments-14C-NationalMuseumofSingapore-20090712.jpg/250px-JavaneseGoldOrnaments-14C-NationalMuseumofSingapore-20090712.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:JavaneseGoldOrnaments-14C-NationalMuseumofSingapore-20090712.jpg) 14th-century gold armlets and rings in East Javanese Majapahit style, found at Fort Canning Hill, [Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore "Singapore"), suggests that [Tumasik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temasek "Temasek") or [Singapura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Singapura "Kingdom of Singapura") was within Majapahit sphere of influence.

For several centuries–since the era of [Srivijaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Srivijaya "Srivijaya") and [Medang Mataram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medang_Kingdom "Medang Kingdom") (circa 10th century), [the classic rivalry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mataram%E2%80%93Srivijayan_wars "Mataram–Srivijayan wars") between Sumatran [Malay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_Malay "Ethnic Malay") states and [Javanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Javanese_people "Javanese people") kingdoms has shaped the dynamics of geopolitics in the region.
Their activities in guarding the sea in pursuit of their economic interest, plus their military activities to safeguard this interests, has led to conflicts between Malays and Javanese.[&#8202;](#ref-back-200-0)[\[178\]](#refnote-200) Since early of its formation, Majapahit inherited the foreign outlook of its predecessor entity – [Singhasari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singhasari "Singhasari") kingdom, in which its last king [Kertanegara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kertanegara "Kertanegara") projected his influence abroad by launching [Pamalayu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pamalayu "Pamalayu") expedition (1275–1293) to incorporate Malay polities in Sumatra and the Malay Peninsula within Javanese sphere of influence. Previously, Singhasari seeks to dominate trade route, especially Malacca strait, and also saw the outreach of Mongolian-Chinese Yuan influence into Southeast Asia as the threat to their interest. During its formation, the returning Pamalayu expedition in 1293 has brought Malayu Kingdoms in Jambi and Dharmasraya under Javanese suzerainty. Therefore, Java often considers itself as the overlord of most of the polities in the Indonesian archipelago.

During the reign of Majapahit's first two monarch–Wijaya and Jayanegara, the kingdom struggled to consolidate its rule, plagued by several rebellions. However, it was not until the reign of its third monarch–Queen Tribhuwana Tunggadewi, and her son, Hayam Wuruk–that the kingdom began to project its power overseas. Majapahit's confidence for dominance was stemmed for their economic and demographic comparative advantage; agrarian as well as a maritime nation; their large rice production, immense human resources, well-organized society, also their mastery in shipbuilding, navigation and military technology; are excellent relatively compared to their neighbours. These strengths were used by Gajah Mada to expand the kingdom's influence and building a maritime empire. This rather imperialistic outlook has projected in the way he forcefully dealt with Majapahit's neighbours; the Pabali (conquest of Bali, 1342–1343) and the [Pasunda Bubat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Bubat "Battle of Bubat") (1356). Majapahit pulled Bali into their orbit as a vassal state. While the disastrous diplomacy with the [Sunda kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunda_Kingdom "Sunda Kingdom") has led to the enmity among them.

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c3/Bhairava_Adityavarman.jpg/250px-Bhairava_Adityavarman.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Bhairava_Adityavarman.jpg) [Adityawarman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adityawarman "Adityawarman"), a senior minister of Majapahit depicted as Bhairava. He established the [Pagaruyung Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pagaruyung_Kingdom "Pagaruyung Kingdom") in Central Sumatra.

Majapahit overlordship upon Malay states of Sumatra was demonstrated by the rule of [Adityawarman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adityawarman "Adityawarman") of Malayupura. Adityawarman, the cousin of King Jayanegara, was raised within Majapahit palace and rose to become a senior minister in Majapahit court. He was sent to led Majapahit military expansion to conquer east coast region in Sumatra. Adityawarman then founded the royal dynasty of [Minangkabau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minangkabau_people "Minangkabau people") in [Pagarruyung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pagarruyung "Pagarruyung") and presided over the central Sumatra region to take control of the gold trade between 1347 and 1375.

Within Indonesian archipelago, Majapahit saw itself as the centre of a huge mandala. This notion is demonstrated by its three-tier administrative hierarchy; Nagara Agung, Mancanegara, and Nusantara.

In Sumatran front, Majapahit did capture Palembang, Jambi and Dharmasraya, invade [Pasai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samudera_Pasai_Sultanate "Samudera Pasai Sultanate"), and it also saw the settlement of [Tumasik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temasek "Temasek"), that later become [Kingdom of Singapura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Singapura "Kingdom of Singapura"), as its rebellious colony, and thus deal with them accordingly.

In later years after the era of Hayam Wuruk, Majapahit has lost their grips on some of their overseas possessions. This led to the thrive and the rise of several polities previously held under Majapahit domination, such as [Brunei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruneian_Empire "Bruneian Empire") and [Malacca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malacca_Sultanate "Malacca Sultanate"). The rise of Malacca in the 15th century, in particular, is important, because it represents Majapahit eventual failure to control Malacca strait. Previously Majapahit tried to contain the rise of a potential regional rival; a Malay polity in par with Srivijaya, by punishing a rebellion in Palembang and capturing Singapura. In this sense, Malacca was indeed the rival of Majapahit in the competition to dominate the archipelagic realm. Despite this perceived rivalry, in practice, however, the two kingdoms had some close and intense economic and cultural relations. At that time, the trade link between Majapahit ports of Hujung Galuh and Tuban with the port of Melaka must have thrived.

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/30/Cast_Chinese_coins_%28330_B.C._-_1912_A.D.%29.jpg/250px-Cast_Chinese_coins_%28330_B.C._-_1912_A.D.%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Cast_Chinese_coins_(330_B.C._-_1912_A.D.).jpg) On centre bottom row (no. 8) is a Yǒng-Lè Tōng-Bǎo (永樂通寶) cash coin cast under the [Yǒng-Lè Emperor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yongle_Emperor "Yongle Emperor") (永樂帝) of Ming dynasty. These were cast in great quantities and used by [Ashikaga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashikaga_Shogunate "Ashikaga Shogunate"), [Ryukyu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ry%C5%ABky%C5%AB_Kingdom "Ryūkyū Kingdom"), as well as Majapahit.

Majapahit true rival for domination, however, was the powerful [Ming Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ming_dynasty "Ming dynasty"). After the fall of Yuan, the Ming Emperor eager to project his power into Southeast Asia. On the other hand, Majapahit saw this archipelagic realm as theirs and unwelcome to any perceived Chinese interference. After Majapahit was weakened by Paregreg civil war, and incessant conflicts among its nobles, the arrival of the formidable [Ming treasure voyages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ming_treasure_voyages "Ming treasure voyages") led by [Zheng He](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zheng_He "Zheng He") upon Majapahit shores has pushed Majapahit prestige and power to the side. Ming on the other hand, actively support the rise of Malacca. This Ming protection has made Majapahit unwilling and unable to push Malacca anymore.[&#8202;](#ref-back-TOI-Sanyal-3)[\[69\]](#refnote-TOI-Sanyal)

Majapahit ultimate nemesis, however, was located very close at home; the [Demak Sultanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demak_Sultanate "Demak Sultanate") on the northern coast of Central Java. Ming support to Malacca, and Malacca active proselytizing of Islam, has led to the thriving and rise of Muslim traders community in the archipelago, including in Majapahit ports on the north coast of Java. This in turn gradually corroded the prestige of Javanese Hindu-Buddhist kingship, and after several generations, led to the fall of once-mighty Majapahit empire.[&#8202;](#ref-back-201-0)[\[179\]](#refnote-201)

## Legacy

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Majapahit&action=edit&section=35 "Edit section: Legacy")\]

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8c/DenpasarPuraMaospahit.JPG/250px-DenpasarPuraMaospahit.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:DenpasarPuraMaospahit.JPG) _Pura Maospahit_ ("Majapahit Temple") in [Denpasar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denpasar "Denpasar"), Bali, demonstrate the typical Majapahit red brick architecture.

Majapahit was the largest empire ever to form in Southeast Asia.[&#8202;](#ref-back-miksic-5)[\[54\]](#refnote-miksic):107 Although its political power beyond the core area in east Java was diffuse, constituting mainly ceremonial recognition of suzerainty, Majapahit society developed a high degree of sophistication in both commercial and artistic activities. Its capital was inhabited by a cosmopolitan population among whom literature and art flourished.[&#8202;](#ref-back-miksic-6)[\[54\]](#refnote-miksic):107

Numbers of local legends and [folklores](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folklore "Folklore") in the region had mentioned about the Majapahit kingdom. Other than Javanese sources, some regional legends mentioning Majapahit kingdom or its general Gajah Mada also can be found; from Aceh, Minangkabau, Palembang, the Malay Peninsula, Sunda, Brunei, Bali to Sumbawa. Most of them mentioned about the incoming Javanese forces to their land, which was probably a local testament of the empire's expansive nature that once dominating the archipelago. The [Hikayat Raja Pasai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hikayat_Raja-raja_Pasai "Hikayat Raja-raja Pasai"), a 14th-century [Aceh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aceh "Aceh") chronicle tell a Majapahit naval invasion against [Samudra Pasai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samudra_Pasai "Samudra Pasai") in 1350. The chronicle described that the Majapahit invasion was a punishment for Sultan Ahmad Malik Az-Zahir's crime on ruining a royal marriage between Pasai Prince Tun Abdul Jalil and Raden Galuh Gemerencang, a Majapahit princess–that led to the death of the royal couple.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Tirto-Pasai-1)[\[43\]](#refnote-Tirto-Pasai)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/85/Masjid_Menara_Kudus.jpg/250px-Masjid_Menara_Kudus.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Masjid_Menara_Kudus.jpg) The Majapahit style minaret of Kudus Mosque

In West Sumatra, the [legend of Minangkabau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minangkabau_(legend) "Minangkabau (legend)") mentioned an invading foreign prince–associated with Javanese Majapahit kingdom–being defeated in a buffalo fight.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Sindo-Minangkabau-0)[\[180\]](#refnote-Sindo-Minangkabau) In [West Java](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Java "West Java"), the [Pasunda Bubat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Bubat "Battle of Bubat") tragedy caused a myth to revolve around Indonesians, which forbids marriage between a [Sundanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sundanese_people "Sundanese people") and a [Javanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Javanese_people "Javanese people"), as it would be unsustainable and only bring misery to the couple.[&#8202;](#ref-back-203-0)[\[181\]](#refnote-203) In Malay Peninsula, the [Malay Annals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_Annals "Malay Annals") mentioned the legend of the fall of [Singapura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Singapura "Kingdom of Singapura") to Majapahit forces in 1398 was due to the betrayal of Sang Rajuna Tapa that opened the fortified city gate.[&#8202;](#ref-back-204-0)[\[182\]](#refnote-204) In [Brunei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brunei "Brunei"), the folk legend of Lumut Lunting and Pilong-Pilongan islands in [Brunei Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brunei_Bay "Brunei Bay") also connected to Majapahit.[&#8202;](#ref-back-205-0)[\[183\]](#refnote-205)

Several Javanese legends were originated or become popular during the Majapahit period. The [Panji cycles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panji_(prince) "Panji (prince)"), the tale of [Sri Tanjung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sri_Tanjung "Sri Tanjung"), and the epic of [Damarwulan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Damarwulan "Damarwulan"), are popular tales in Javanese and [Balinese literatures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balinese_literature "Balinese literature"). The tales of Panji was dated from the older period during Kediri kingdom, while the tale of Sri Tanjung and the epic of Damarwulan took place during the Majapahit period. These tales remained a popular theme in Javanese culture of later period during Mataram Sultanate, and often became the source of inspiration for [wayang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayang "Wayang") shadow puppet performance, [ketoprak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ketoprak "Ketoprak") and [topeng](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Topeng "Topeng") dance drama. The Panji tales, in particular, have spread from East Java to become a source of inspiration for literature and dance drama throughout the region, as far as the Malay Peninsula, Cambodia and Siam where he is known as Raden Inao or Enau ( [Thai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_language "Thai language"): อิเหนา) of Kurepan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEHolt1967274-0)[\[184\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEHolt1967274)

Majapahit had a momentous and lasting influence on [Indonesian art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_art "Indonesian art") and [architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_architecture "Indonesian architecture"). The empire's expansion circa 14th-century contributed to the diffusion Javanese cultural influence throughout the archipelago, which can be seen as a form of [Javanisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Javanisation "Javanisation"). It was probably during this period that some of the Javanese cultural elements, such as [gamelan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamelan "Gamelan")[&#8202;](#ref-back-207-0)[\[185\]](#refnote-207) and kris, being expanded and introduced to islands outside of Java. The descriptions of the architecture of the capital's pavilions ( _[pendopo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pendopo "Pendopo")_) in the _Nagarakretagama_ evoke the Javanese [Kraton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kraton_(Indonesia) "Kraton (Indonesia)") also the [Balinese temples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pura_(Balinese_temple) "Pura (Balinese temple)") and palace compounds of today. The Majapahit architectural style that often employs [terracotta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majapahit_Terracotta "Majapahit Terracotta") and [red brick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_brick "Red brick") heavily influenced the architecture of Java and Bali in the later period. The Majapahit style _[candi bentar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Candi_bentar "Candi bentar")_ split gate, the _kori_ or _[paduraksa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paduraksa "Paduraksa")_ towering red-brick gate, and also _pendopo_ pavilion have become ubiquitous in Javanese and Balinese architectural features, as seen in [Menara Kudus Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Menara_Kudus_Mosque "Menara Kudus Mosque"), [Keraton Kasepuhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keraton_Kasepuhan "Keraton Kasepuhan") and Sunyaragi park in [Cirebon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cirebon "Cirebon"), [Mataram Sultanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mataram_Sultanate "Mataram Sultanate") royal cemetery in [Kota Gede](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kota_Gede "Kota Gede"), [Yogyakarta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yogyakarta "Yogyakarta"), and various palaces and temples in Bali.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Made_Wijaya-1)[\[94\]](#refnote-Made_Wijaya)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/76/COLLECTIE_TROPENMUSEUM_Bas-reli%C3%ABfs_op_het_Pendopo_Terras_Panataran_tempelcomplex_TMnr_20027152.jpg/250px-COLLECTIE_TROPENMUSEUM_Bas-reli%C3%ABfs_op_het_Pendopo_Terras_Panataran_tempelcomplex_TMnr_20027152.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:COLLECTIE_TROPENMUSEUM_Bas-reli%C3%ABfs_op_het_Pendopo_Terras_Panataran_tempelcomplex_TMnr_20027152.jpg) Bas relief from [Candi Penataran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Candi_Penataran "Candi Penataran") describes the Javanese-style [pendopo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pendopo "Pendopo") pavilion, commonly found across Java and Bali.

The vivid, rich and festive [Balinese culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balinese_people#Culture "Balinese people") is considered one of Majapahit's legacy. The Javanese Hindu civilisation since the era of [Airlangga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airlangga "Airlangga") to the era of Majapahit kings has profoundly influenced and shaped the Balinese culture and [history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Bali "History of Bali").[&#8202;](#ref-back-208-0)[\[186\]](#refnote-208) The ancient links and Majapahit legacy is observable in many ways; architecture, literature, religious rituals, dance-drama and artforms. The aesthetics and style of bas-reliefs in Majapahit East Javanese temples were preserved and copied in Balinese temples. It is also because, after the fall of the empire, many Majapahit nobles, artisans and priests had taken refuge either in the interior mountainous region of East Java or across the narrow strait to Bali. Indeed, in some ways, the [Kingdom of Bali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bali_Kingdom "Bali Kingdom") was the successor of Majapahit. Large numbers of Majapahit manuscripts, such as [Nagarakretagama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nagarakretagama "Nagarakretagama"), [Sutasoma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kakawin_Sutasoma "Kakawin Sutasoma"), [Pararaton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pararaton "Pararaton") and [Tantu Pagelaran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tantu_Pagelaran "Tantu Pagelaran"), were being well-kept in royal libraries of Bali and Lombok and provides the glimpse and valuable historical records on Majapahit. The Majapahit Hindu-Javanese culture has shaped the culture of Bali, that led to popular expression; "without Java, there is no Bali". Yet in return, Bali is credited as the last stronghold to safeguard and preserve the ancient Hindu Javanese civilisation.

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d5/WLANL_-_mchangsp_-_Kris_van_Knaud.jpg/250px-WLANL_-_mchangsp_-_Kris_van_Knaud.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:WLANL_-_mchangsp_-_Kris_van_Knaud.jpg) The [Kris of Knaud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kris_of_Knaud "Kris of Knaud"), one of the oldest surviving kris, is dated to Majapahit period.

In weaponry, the Majapahit expansion is believed to be responsible for the widespread use of the [keris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kris "Kris") dagger in Southeast Asia; from Java, Bali, Sumatra, Malaysia, Brunei to Southern Thailand. Although it has been suggested that the keris, and native daggers similar to it, predate Majapahit, nevertheless the empire expansion contributed to its popularity and diffusion in the region around the year 1492.[&#8202;](#ref-back-JPostKris-0)[\[187\]](#refnote-JPostKris) For example, [Kris of Knaud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kris_of_Knaud "Kris of Knaud"), one of the oldest surviving kris is dated to 1264 Śaka (which correspondents to 1342). The Malay legend of Kris [Taming Sari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taming_Sari "Taming Sari") is also attributed to Majapahit origin.

For Indonesians in later centuries, Majapahit became a symbol of past greatness. The Islamic sultanates of [Demak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demak_Sultanate "Demak Sultanate"), [Pajang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pajang "Pajang"), and [Mataram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mataram_Sultanate "Mataram Sultanate") sought to establish their legitimacy to the Majapahit.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ricklefs-9)[\[11\]](#refnote-ricklefs):40 The Demak claimed a line of succession through Kertabumi, as its founder [Raden Patah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raden_Patah "Raden Patah"), in court chronicles was said to be the son of Kertabumi with Putri Cina, a Chinese princess, who had been sent away before her son was born.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ricklefs-10)[\[11\]](#refnote-ricklefs):36–37[Sultan Agung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultan_Agung "Sultan Agung")'s conquest of [Wirasaba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wirasaba?action=edit&redlink=1 "Wirasaba (page does not exist)") (present-day Mojoagung) in 1615–during that time just a small town without significant strategic and economic value–led by the sultan himself, may probably have had such symbolic importance as it was the location of the former Majapahit capital.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ricklefs-11)[\[11\]](#refnote-ricklefs):43 Central Javanese palaces have traditions and [genealogy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genealogy "Genealogy") that attempt to prove links back to the Majapahit royal lines–usually in the form of a grave as a vital _link_ in Java – where legitimacy is enhanced by such a connection.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\] Bali, in particular, was heavily influenced by Majapahit and the [Balinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balinese_people "Balinese people") consider themselves to be the true heirs of the kingdom.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Schoppert1997-1)[\[93\]](#refnote-Schoppert1997)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/62/Relief_of_Indonesian_History%2C_Monas.JPG/250px-Relief_of_Indonesian_History%2C_Monas.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Relief_of_Indonesian_History,_Monas.JPG) The high reliefs of Gajah Mada and Majapahit history depicted in [Monas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Monument_(Indonesia) "National Monument (Indonesia)") has become the source of Indonesian national pride of past greatness.

Modern Indonesian nationalists, including those of the early 20th-century [Indonesian National Revival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_National_Revival#National_Revival "Indonesian National Revival"), have invoked the Majapahit Empire.[&#8202;](#ref-back-M_Wood-2)[\[9\]](#refnote-M_Wood) Indonesian founding fathers—especially [Sukarno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukarno "Sukarno") and [Mohammad Yamin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Yamin "Mohammad Yamin"), built a historical construct around Majapahit to argue for the ancient unified realm, as a predecessor of modern Indonesia.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NG-Majapahit-0)[\[188\]](#refnote-NG-Majapahit) The memory of its greatness remains in Indonesia and is sometimes seen as a precedent for the current political boundaries of the Republic.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ricklefs-12)[\[11\]](#refnote-ricklefs):19 Many of modern Indonesian national symbols derived from Majapahit Hindu-Buddhist elements. The Indonesian national motto, " _[Bhinneka Tunggal Ika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhinneka_Tunggal_Ika "Bhinneka Tunggal Ika")_", is a quotation from an [Old Javanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Javanese "Old Javanese") poem " _Kakawin Sutasoma_", written by a Majapahit poet, Mpu Tantular.[&#8202;](#ref-back-211-0)[\[189\]](#refnote-211)

The Indonesian coat of arms, _[Garuda Pancasila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coat_of_arms_of_Indonesia "Coat of arms of Indonesia")_, also derives from [Javanese Hindu elements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism_in_Indonesia "Hinduism in Indonesia"). The statue and relief of [Garuda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garuda "Garuda") have been found in many temples in Java such as [Prambanan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prambanan "Prambanan") from the ancient [Mataram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mataram_Kingdom "Mataram Kingdom") era, and the [Panataran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penataran_Temple "Penataran Temple") as well as the [Sukuh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Candi_Sukuh "Candi Sukuh") temple dated from the Majapahit era. The notable statue of Garuda is the statue of the king [Airlangga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airlangga "Airlangga") depicted as [Vishnu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vishnu "Vishnu") riding [Garuda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garuda "Garuda").

In its propaganda from the 1920s, the [Communist Party of Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_Indonesia "Communist Party of Indonesia") presented its vision of a classless society as a reincarnation of a romanticised Majapahit.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ricklefs-13)[\[11\]](#refnote-ricklefs):174 It was invoked by Sukarno for nation building and by the [New Order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orde_Baru "Orde Baru") as an expression of state expansion and consolidation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-212-0)[\[190\]](#refnote-212) Like Majapahit, the modern state of Indonesia covers vast territory and is politically centred on Java.

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ad/Patih_Gajah_Mada.jpg/250px-Patih_Gajah_Mada.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Patih_Gajah_Mada.jpg) Gajah Mada statue in front of the now-demolished Telecommunication Museum in [Taman Mini Indonesia Indah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taman_Mini_Indonesia_Indah "Taman Mini Indonesia Indah"), Jakarta. [Palapa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palapa "Palapa"), Indonesia's first telecommunication satellite launched on 9 July 1976 was named after [Palapa oath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palapa_oath "Palapa oath").

[Palapa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palapa "Palapa"), the series of communication satellites owned by [Telkom Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telkom_Indonesia "Telkom Indonesia"), an Indonesian telecommunication company, was named after _Sumpah Palapa_, the famous oath taken by [Gajah Mada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gajah_Mada "Gajah Mada"), who swore that he would not taste any spice as long as he had not succeeded in unifying Nusantara (Indonesian archipelago). This ancient oath of unification signifies the Palapa satellite as the modern means to unify the Indonesian archipelago by way of [telecommunication](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telecommunication "Telecommunication"). The name was chosen by the president [Suharto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suharto "Suharto"), and the program was started in February 1975.

Pura Kawitan Majapahit was built in 1995 as a homage to honour the empire that inspires the nation. Majapahit is often regarded as the antecedent of the modern state of Indonesia. This Hindu temple complex is located within Trowulan just north of Segaran pool.[&#8202;](#ref-back-213-0)[\[191\]](#refnote-213)

During the last half-year of 2008, the Indonesian government sponsored a massive exploration on the site that is believed to be the place where the palace of Majapahit once stood. [Jero Wacik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jero_Wacik "Jero Wacik"), the Indonesian Minister of Culture and Tourism stated that the [Majapahit Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trowulan#The_Majapahit_Park "Trowulan") would be built on the site and completed as early as 2009, to prevent further damage caused by home-made brick industries that developed in the surrounding area.[&#8202;](#ref-back-214-0)[\[192\]](#refnote-214) Nevertheless, the project leaves a huge attention to some historians, since constructing the park's foundation in Segaran site located in south side of [Trowulan Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trowulan_Museum "Trowulan Museum") will inevitably damage the site itself. Ancient bricks which are historically valuable were found scattered on the site. The government then argued that the method they were applying were less destructive since digging method were used instead of drilling.[&#8202;](#ref-back-215-0)[\[193\]](#refnote-215)

## List of rulers

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Majapahit&action=edit&section=36 "Edit section: List of rulers")\]

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/44/Rajasa_Dynasty.svg/250px-Rajasa_Dynasty.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Rajasa_Dynasty.svg) Genealogy diagram of the [Rajasa dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajasa_dynasty "Rajasa dynasty"), the royal family of Singhasari and Majapahit. Rulers are highlighted with period of reign.

The rulers of Majapahit belong to the [Rajasa dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajasa_dynasty "Rajasa dynasty"), which was founded in the early 13th century by [Sri Ranggah Rajasa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ken_Arok "Ken Arok"), the first king of [Singhasari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singhasari "Singhasari").[&#8202;](#ref-back-:022-1)[\[116\]](#refnote-:022)

| Reign | Monarch | Capital | Information and events |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1293–1309 | [Saṅgrāmavijaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raden_Wijaya "Raden Wijaya"), styled Kṛtarājasa Jayavardhana[&#8202;](#ref-back-Coedes-11)[\[32\]](#refnote-Coedes):201 | [Trowulan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trowulan "Trowulan") | - Victory against [Mongol forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_invasion_of_Java "Mongol invasion of Java") sent by [Kublai Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kublai_Khan "Kublai Khan") of the [Yuan dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuan_dynasty "Yuan dynasty") (1293).<br>- Ascended to Javanese throne on the 15th Kārttika of 1215 Śaka (10 November 1293).<br>- Ranggalawe rebellion (1295). |
| 1309–1328 | [Jayanagara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jayanegara "Jayanegara"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-Coedes-12)[\[32\]](#refnote-Coedes):233–234 styled Sundarapāṇḍyadevādhīśvara | [Trowulan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trowulan "Trowulan") | - Nambi rebellion (1316).<br>- Kuti rebellion (1319).<br>- Jayanagara's assassination by Tanca (1328). |
| 1328–1350s | Gitārjā, styled [Tribhuvanottuṅgadevī Jayaviṣṇuvardhanī](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tribhuwana_Wijayatunggadewi "Tribhuwana Wijayatunggadewi")[&#8202;](#ref-back-Coedes-13)[\[32\]](#refnote-Coedes):234 | [Trowulan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trowulan "Trowulan") | - Sadeng and Keta rebellion (1331).<br>- _Pabali_, the conquest of [Bali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bali_Kingdom "Bali Kingdom") (1342–1343).<br>- Conquest of [Samudra Pasai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samudra_Pasai "Samudra Pasai") (1349–1350). |
| 1350s–1389/1399[&#8202;](#ref-back-216-0)[\[194\]](#refnote-216) | [Hayam Wuruk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hayam_Wuruk "Hayam Wuruk"), styled Rājasanagara[&#8202;](#ref-back-Coedes-14)[\[32\]](#refnote-Coedes):239 | [Trowulan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trowulan "Trowulan") | - [Pasunda Bubat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Bubat "Battle of Bubat") (1357).<br>- Majapahit golden age described in the _[Nagarakṛtāgama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nagarakretagama "Nagarakretagama")_ (1365).<br>- Punitive attack on the Palembang rebellion (1377). |
| 1389/1399–1429 | [Vikramavardhana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vikramavardhana "Vikramavardhana"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-Coedes-15)[\[32\]](#refnote-Coedes):241 styled Bhaṭāra Viśeṣa | [Trowulan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trowulan "Trowulan") | - Siege and invasion of [Singapura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Singapura "Kingdom of Singapura") (1398).<br>- [Paregreg civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paregreg_war "Paregreg war") (1405–1406), Vikramavardhana defeating Bhre Vīrabhūmi.<br>- Campaign against [Pagarruyung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pagarruyung "Pagarruyung") (1409). |
| 1429–1447 | [Suhitā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suhita "Suhita")[&#8202;](#ref-back-Coedes-16)[\[32\]](#refnote-Coedes):242 | [Trowulan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trowulan "Trowulan") |  |
| 1447–1451 | [Kṛtavijaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kertawijaya "Kertawijaya"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-Coedes-17)[\[32\]](#refnote-Coedes):242 styled Vijayaparākramavardhana | [Trowulan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trowulan "Trowulan") |  |
| 1451–1453 | Vijayakumāra, styled Rājasavardhana[&#8202;](#ref-back-Coedes-18)[\[32\]](#refnote-Coedes):242 | [Trowulan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trowulan "Trowulan") |  |
| 1453–1456 | [Interregnum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interregnum "Interregnum") |  |  |
| 1456–1466 | Sūryavikrama, styled Girīśavardhana[&#8202;](#ref-back-Coedes-19)[\[32\]](#refnote-Coedes):242 | [Trowulan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trowulan "Trowulan") |  |
| 1466–1478 | Suraprabhāva,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Coedes-20)[\[32\]](#refnote-Coedes):242 styled Siṅhavikramavardhana | [Trowulan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trowulan "Trowulan") |  |
| c. 1486–1527 | [Girīndravardhana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Girindrawardhana "Girindrawardhana") | [Trowulan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trowulan "Trowulan") | - [Demak-Majapahit conflicts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demak-Majapahit_conflicts "Demak-Majapahit conflicts") (1478-1527). |
